ADAEZE'S ORCHARD

A flood canal that would keep twenty thousand people dry has to cross the one hillside Illuminara's mother will not give up, and the rains are twenty-one weeks away. Her mother will not say why. Illuminara has to learn what a refusal is carrying, and whether anyone's explanation of it is right.

01 Â· A Reasonable No

Okafor orchard, Kestrel Hill Â· Day One, 07:10

The orchard smelled of guavas the pickers had missed, sweet and a little rotten, and Corinna Saleh had pinned her plans to the hood of the authority's truck so the wind could not read them. Below the lower wall the town of Marrow Ford lay in its river bend, twenty thousand people on ground that had flooded twice in living memory and would flood again in twenty-one weeks.

The diversion was easy to say. When the river crested, a canal across Kestrel Saddle would carry thirty-eight cubic metres a second into the old quarry for the six hours the crest lasted, and the lower town would keep its streets. The saddle's lowest line ran through the third terrace of Adaeze Okafor's orchard.

Corinna SalehThe north route misses you. So does the south. Fourteen months and sixteen. We have twenty-one weeks.

Adaeze came down between the rows with a basket on her hip, small and straight-backed, and read the plans the way a woman reads a bill.

Adaeze OkaforNo.

Corinna SalehThe authority can compel, Mrs Okafor. It would rather not.

Adaeze OkaforThen compel. You will need a magistrate and a year. I have the year. Ask my daughter how much you have.

Illuminara had two alignments in her head that missed the orchard entirely, and she knew the price of each in months. She had come up the hill ready to argue. She found a wall instead, and her first thought was that walls like this are built out of grief and inheritance, four generations of both. She filed the refusal there. It was the first mistake.

IlluminaraMama. Why?

Adaeze OkaforYou are making the face, Nara. The one that has already decided what I mean.

IlluminaraThen tell me what you mean.

Adaeze OkaforWalk the hill with me. Not with her, and not with a clipboard. Wednesday, early. Wear shoes.

Corinna wrote the date on the back of her hand. Adaeze gave her a guava, and neither of them seemed to know what the other had won.

02 Â· What the Hill Carries

Okafor orchard, third terrace Â· Day Three, 06:00

Illuminara had two ways of walking a hill. The ordinary one used her feet. The other let the slope's weight come up through her soles as a map: where the soil leaned on the clay, where the clay leaned on the stone, ten thousand tons settling downhill as quietly as a held breath, every load finding its road to the ground. It told her where force went. It never told her why.

Under the third terrace the weight did something it should not have done. It ran in a line.

She stopped between two trees. Adaeze, ahead of her with the basket, stopped without turning round.

Adaeze OkaforFound it.

IlluminaraThere is a seam under here. Forty metres, perfectly straight, four metres down. Rock does not do straight.

Adaeze OkaforMy father said never cut across the third terrace. He never said why, and he was not a man you asked twice. I plant where his grandmother planted. The trees hold the hill, Nara. That is all I know and all I will swear to.

Illuminara knelt and put her palm on the wet grass. Roots go a metre down, perhaps a little more, and the seam lay four below that. She said so as gently as the fact allowed, and Adaeze took it the way she took weather.

Adaeze OkaforThen it is deeper, and my trees are still standing on it.

The next idea arrived whole, the way good ones do, and Illuminara liked it at once. The terraces already stepped down the hill like a staircase built for water.

IlluminaraLine the lower three terraces and let the flood run down them. The cut never touches the third terrace. Every tree stays.

Adaeze OkaforNo.

IlluminaraYou have not heard the numbers.

Adaeze OkaforA guava can stand three hours of wet feet. You are offering it six, on top of ground that has to drain afterwards, not hold. Those lower rows live because the water goes through this hill. You want to put the river on top of what my family sent underneath.

It was the second no, and it was not sentiment. Illuminara had been designing around the orchard as if it were a surface to be spared while the engineering happened somewhere else. Adaeze was telling her that the orchard was the engineering.

IlluminaraUnderneath where?

Adaeze led her up to a stone lid sunk in the grass above the third terrace, and then down, a long way down, to a hole the size of a fist in the lower wall, where a thread of water ticked onto a flat stone.

Adaeze OkaforThe spring box. And that is the drip. My father's drain. Nobody has cleaned it since he died.

IlluminaraMama, that is the seam.

Adaeze OkaforThat is a drain. A drain is a pipe for the trees' feet.

IlluminaraA drain forty metres long, laid straight under the heaviest part of the hill, with the hill leaning on it. I do not think it is only for the trees.

03 Â· Three Predictions

Okafor orchard, sixth terrace Â· Day Five, 16:20

Tomas Reyes unrolled sensor cable behind his truck like a man feeding out line to a very long, very patient animal. He was the authority's geotechnical engineer, a heavy, careful person who had spent a career being right about hills, and he was not offended by a sprinkler test. He was only unconvinced. Corinna stood by the tailgate with her arms folded and the date still faint on her hand.

Idowu Bello had walked over from the next plot with his hands in his pockets. He had cleared his half of the hillside in a dry year and sold it to the authority in the spring, and he looked at the bare slope the way a man looks at a decision.

Idowu BelloUse my ground. There is nothing on it to protect, and I would like to know whether I should be nervous.

Tomas wrote the predictions on the back of the authority's own form, so that no one could say later that they had been fitted to the result.

Tomas ReyesMine first. This hill is fractured sandstone. Water leaves through the cracks wherever you put it, so the orchard, your father's drain and Idowu's bare ground will all behave alike. Three plots, one needle.

Adaeze OkaforMine is shorter. Trees hold. The plots with trees stay quiet. Idowu's goes.

IlluminaraMine splits both of yours. The drain holds the hill. The plot on the drain line stays quiet. A plot with trees but no working drain goes with Idowu's ground.

Adaeze considered the slope, and Illuminara watched her decide to be generous.

Adaeze OkaforThe sixth terrace, east corner. The inlet silted the year of the big wind. Water stands there every spring, and I have never got a good guava out of it.

IlluminaraYou could have told me that on Monday.

Adaeze OkaforYou did not ask me a question on Monday. You told me a plan.

They settled the stakes without anyone raising a voice.

Adaeze OkaforIf Tomas is right, I sign the saddle cut myself. If I am right, you find another road. If my daughter is right, she says so to my face, and then she cleans my drain.

Corinna SalehOne condition from me. If the drain line crosses ten, the saddle is dead and I take the south route, sixteen months, and I tell the town today that the rains will find it unprotected.

Corinna SalehEvery day on this is a day the rains do not give back.

IlluminaraFour days. And Tomas has just written you a geotechnical report on the back of a form.

04 Â· The Needle

Okafor orchard, three plots Â· Day Nine, 13:05

The sprinklers were set to the storm the hill sees once in a century. Tomas stood over his needle with a flask of tea. Illuminara tipped a cup of green dye into the spring box and put the lid back, and Adaeze carried a bucket down to the drip and sat on it with her hand flat on the wall.

At thirty-eight minutes a green thread came out of the hole in the stone. Adaeze looked at it for a while.

Adaeze OkaforA kettle and a half.

The needle on the drain line never passed six. The corner of the sixth terrace, with its trees standing in it, crossed ten at ninety-five minutes, which was where Tomas had said the hill stopped being sure of itself. Idowu's bare ground crossed it at eighty-five. At a hundred and five a painted stake on Idowu's slope moved four millimetres, and Tomas shut off the water with a flat hand, like a man stopping an orchestra.

Tomas ReyesThe drain line was back to two forty minutes after the water stopped. The corner went over ten with the trees in it. Bedrock is out. Same sandstone under all three and they do not agree. I was wrong, and I am glad to be wrong in a way I can write down.

Adaeze OkaforMy trees did not hold it.

Illuminara crouched beside her.

Adaeze OkaforDo not be kind. Be accurate.

IlluminaraYour trees did not hold the hill. Your father's drain did, and your trees have been drinking from it for eighty years. That is why the fruit is good.

Adaeze OkaforThat is kind and accurate. I will allow it.

Illuminara had drawn it in the truck cab on Day Seven, on the chance that she would be allowed to use it. She unrolled it on the bucket lid.

IlluminaraI cannot cut the third terrace and I will not drown your rows. So the canal goes in the bottom seven metres of the orchard, along the wall, in precast panels. The drip comes out into it through a one-way flap. In flood the canal cannot push water back up your father's drain, and in dry months the drain runs out into the canal as it always has. Tomas clears the silted inlet in the sixth corner. Where the flood outgrows the quarry, a weir at the head of the canal puts the surplus back in the river below the town, where Corinna's survey says the channel can take it.

Adaeze OkaforSeven metres of what? How many trees?

IlluminaraNine trees stand in the strip. I can save two, and you choose which.

Adaeze OkaforSeven, then, and I choose the seven. If the flood ever needs more than the strip, you ask me, and if I say no, it is no.

IlluminaraThe design works on the strip alone. I made it so it would still work if you refused me everything else.

Adaeze OkaforGood. And I plant the bank. Nobody else touches a root.

Corinna SalehThe strip is not in the permit.

IlluminaraClause fourteen lets you vary the line by four hundred metres where the owner consents and a geotechnical report supports it. You have a consenting owner and a report on a form.

Corinna SalehSeventeen weeks to build. You have nineteen.

IlluminaraTwo weeks of slack.

Corinna SalehAnd rain does not read calendars.

05 Â· The Drip

Okafor orchard, new bank Â· Month Five, the first night of the rains

The last panel went in eight days before the rain. Along the lower wall stood seven pale stumps that Adaeze had chosen herself, oldest first, and had not allowed anyone to photograph.

The river began to rise at 01:40. Corinna's voice came out of the control hut radio, flat and exact, and at 02:05 she said the gates were open. The canal filled with a sound like a long breath let out. At the foot of the wall the flap on the drip clapped shut once under the pressure, and the thread of water stopped.

Adaeze stood under an umbrella that was not helping and took her daughter's arm without looking, with the grip of a woman checking a rope. Below them, Marrow Ford kept its lights on.

Corinna SalehBasin at half. Spill weir shut. Lower town is dry, every street.

Eight hundred and twenty thousand cubic metres went into the old quarry before the crest passed at a little after eight. It had room for eleven hundred thousand. The spill weir never opened.

When the canal began to drain, the flap lifted by itself. For a moment nothing happened, and then the stone under the wall began to tick.

Adaeze OkaforIt's running.

In the grey light Adaeze knelt on the new bank with a tray of rooted cuttings she had taken from the oldest of the seven before it came down. Illuminara held the pot while she settled the first one in.

Adaeze OkaforStraight, Nara. You still do not call before you come.

IlluminaraNo.

Adaeze OkaforGood. Then I will keep the kettle on.

03 Â· Three Predictions

Okafor orchard, sixth terrace Â· Day Five, 16:20

Tomas Reyes unrolled sensor cable behind his truck like a man feeding out line to a very long, very patient animal. He was the authority's geotechnical engineer, a heavy, careful person who had spent a career being right about hills, and he was not offended by a sprinkler test. He was only unconvinced. Idowu BelloUse my ground. There is nothing on it to protect, and I would like to know whether I should be nervous.

Tomas wrote the predictions on the back of the authority's own form, so that no one could say later that they had been fitted to the result.

Tomas ReyesMine first. This hill is fractured sandstone. Water leaves through the cracks wherever you put it, so the orchard, your father's drain and Idowu's bare ground will all behave alike. The needles read pore pressure in kilopascals, and my model says the slope moves at ten. On the drain plot I set a twin needle six metres off the line, same stone, same depth. If I am right, the twins read alike.

Adaeze OkaforMine is shorter. Trees hold. The plots with trees stay quiet. Idowu's goes.

IlluminaraMine splits both of yours. The drain holds the hill. The needle on the drain line stays quiet, and its twin, six metres away in the same stone, does not. A plot with trees but no working drain goes with Idowu's ground.

Adaeze considered the slope, and Illuminara watched her decide to be generous.

Adaeze OkaforThe sixth terrace, east corner. The inlet silted the year of the big wind. Water stands there every spring, and I have never got a good guava out of it.

IlluminaraYou could have told me that on Monday.

Adaeze OkaforYou did not ask me a question on Monday. You told me a plan.

They settled the stakes without anyone raising a voice.

Adaeze OkaforIf Tomas is right, I sign the saddle cut myself. If I am right, you find another road. If my daughter is right, she says so to my face, and then she cleans my drain.

Corinna SalehOne condition from me. If the drain line crosses ten, the saddle is dead and I take the south route, sixteen months, and I tell the town today that the rains will find it unprotected. And if a test pour leaves under half a metre of freeboard, I tell them that day too.

Corinna SalehEvery day on this is a day the rains do not give back.

IlluminaraFour days. And Tomas has just written you a geotechnical report on the back of a form.

04 Â· The Needle

Okafor orchard, three plots Â· Day Nine, 13:05

The sprinklers were set to the storm the hill sees once in a century. Tomas stood over his needle with a flask of tea. Illuminara tipped a cup of green dye into the spring box and put the lid back, and Adaeze carried a bucket down to the drip and sat on it with her hand flat on the wall.

At thirty-eight minutes a green thread came out of the hole in the stone. Adaeze looked at it for a while.

Adaeze OkaforA kettle and a half.

The needle on the drain line never passed six kilopascals. Its twin, six metres off the line in the same stone at the same depth, climbed past nine. The corner of the sixth terrace, with its trees standing in it, crossed ten at ninety-five minutes, which was where Tomas had said the hill stopped being sure of itself. Idowu's bare ground crossed it at eighty-five. At a hundred and five a painted stake on Idowu's slope moved four millimetres, and Tomas shut off the water with a flat hand, like a man stopping an orchestra.

Tomas ReyesThe drain line was back to two forty minutes after the water stopped. Its twin was still above six. Fractured sandstone says those two match, and they do not. The corner went over ten with the trees in it. Bedrock is out. I was wrong, and I am glad to be wrong in a way I can write down.

Adaeze OkaforMy trees did not hold it.

Illuminara crouched beside her.

Adaeze OkaforDo not be kind. Be accurate.

IlluminaraYour trees did not hold the hill. Your father's drain did, and your trees have been drinking from it for eighty years. That is why the fruit is good.

Adaeze OkaforThat is kind and accurate. I will allow it.

Illuminara had drawn it in the truck cab on Day Seven, on the chance that she would be allowed to use it. She unrolled it on the bucket lid.

IlluminaraI cannot cut the third terrace and I will not drown your rows. So the canal goes in the bottom seven metres of the orchard, along the wall, in precast panels. The drip comes out into it through a one-way flap. In flood the canal cannot push water back up your father's drain, and in dry months the drain runs out into the canal as it always has. Tomas clears the silted inlet in the sixth corner. Where the flood outgrows the quarry, a weir at the head of the canal returns the surplus to the river below the town.

Tomas ReyesFive metres of bed between concrete walls, two of water: ten square metres. Thirty-eight through ten is three point eight metres a second on a fall of one in four hundred. Panels take that. Soil would not, so no water touches the bank, and a metre of freeboard stands above the surface.

Corinna SalehAnd past thirty-eight?

Tomas ReyesThe weir hands it to the river below the town. That channel takes ten more before it tops its bank, and the worst flood in your survey peaks at forty-six.

Adaeze OkaforSeven metres of what? How many trees?

IlluminaraNine trees stand in the strip. I can save two, and you choose which.

Adaeze OkaforSeven, then, and I choose the seven. If the flood ever needs more than the strip, you ask me, and if I say no, it is no.

IlluminaraThe design works on the strip alone. I made it so it would still work if you refused me everything else.

Adaeze OkaforGood. And I plant the bank. Nobody else touches a root.

Adaeze OkaforYour flap is a door nobody can open. Every October my father poured the spring through the drain from the box above the third terrace and flushed it clean. Shut a flap on that and the silt stays in the pipe, and in five years you have my sixth-corner inlet, forty metres long.

Illuminara looked at the flap on her drawing. She had shown the water how to leave and had not shown anyone how to keep it leaving.

IlluminaraA chain. The flap lifts on a chain run up a sleeve to a hatch in the bank, and the flush goes through when you pull it.

Adaeze OkaforWhen I pull it. In October. And nobody else touches the chain.

Illuminara redrew the flap in pencil on the bucket lid, with her mother's hand on the corner to keep the paper flat.

Corinna SalehThe strip is not in the permit.

IlluminaraClause fourteen lets you vary the line by four hundred metres where the owner consents and a geotechnical report supports it. You have a consenting owner and a report on a form.

Corinna SalehSeventeen weeks to build. You have nineteen.

IlluminaraTwo weeks of slack.

Corinna SalehAnd rain does not read calendars.

05 Â· The Drip

Okafor orchard, new bank Â· Month Five, the first night of the rains

The last panel went in eight days before the rain. Along the lower wall stood seven pale stumps that Adaeze had chosen herself, oldest first, and had not allowed anyone to photograph.

The river began to rise at 01:40. Corinna's voice came out of the control hut radio, flat and exact, and at 02:05 she said the gates were open. Adaeze stood under an umbrella that was not helping and took her daughter's arm without looking, with the grip of a woman checking a rope. Below them, Marrow Ford kept its lights on.

Corinna SalehBasin at half. Spill weir shut. Lower town is dry, every street.

Eight hundred and twenty thousand cubic metres went into the old quarry before the crest passed at a little after eight. It had room for eleven hundred thousand. The spill weir never opened.

When the canal began to drain, the flap lifted by itself. For a moment nothing happened, and then the stone under the wall began to tick. A chain ran from the flap up its sleeve to a hatch in the new bank, and the first October flush was already in Adaeze's planting plan, in her own hand.

Adaeze OkaforIt's running.

In the grey light Adaeze knelt on the new bank with a tray of rooted cuttings she had taken from the oldest of the seven before it came down. Illuminara held the pot while she settled the first one in.

Adaeze OkaforStraight, Nara. You still do not call before you come.

IlluminaraNo.

Adaeze OkaforGood. Then I will keep the kettle on.

Okafor orchard, third terrace Â· Day Three, 06:00

Illuminara had two ways of walking a hill. The ordinary one used her feet. The other let the slope's weight come up through her soles as a map: where the soil leaned on the clay, where the clay leaned on the stone, ten thousand tons settling downhill as quietly as a held breath, every load finding its road to the ground. It told her where force went. It never told her why.

Under the third terrace the weight did something it should not have done. It ran in a line.

She stopped between two trees. Adaeze, ahead of her with the basket, stopped without turning round.

Adaeze OkaforFound it.

IlluminaraThere is a seam under here. Forty metres, perfectly straight, four metres down. Rock does not do straight.

Adaeze OkaforMy father said never cut across the third terrace. He never said why, and he was not a man you asked twice. I plant where his grandmother planted. The trees hold the hill, Nara. That is all I know and all I will swear to.

Illuminara knelt and put her palm on the wet grass. Roots go a metre down, perhaps a little more, and the seam lay four below that. She said so as gently as the fact allowed, and Adaeze took it the way she took weather.

Adaeze OkaforThen it is deeper, and my trees are still standing on it.

The next idea arrived whole, the way good ones do, and Illuminara liked it at once. The terraces already stepped down the hill like a staircase built for water.

IlluminaraLine the lower three terraces and let the flood run down them. The cut never touches the third terrace. Every tree stays.

Adaeze OkaforNo.

IlluminaraYou have not heard the numbers.

Adaeze OkaforA guava can stand three hours of wet feet. You are offering it six, on top of ground that has to drain afterwards, not hold. Those lower rows live because the water goes through this hill. You want to put the river on top of what my family sent underneath.

It was the second no, and it was not sentiment. Illuminara had been designing around the orchard as if it were a surface to be spared while the engineering happened somewhere else. Adaeze was telling her that the orchard was the engineering.

IlluminaraUnderneath where?

Adaeze led her up to a stone lid sunk in the grass above the third terrace, and then down, a long way down, to a hole the size of a fist in the lower wall, where a thread of water ticked onto a flat stone.

Adaeze OkaforThe spring box. And that is the drip. My father's drain. Nobody has cleaned it since he died.

IlluminaraMama, that is the seam.

Adaeze OkaforThat is a drain. A drain is a pipe for the trees' feet.

IlluminaraA drain forty metres long, laid straight under the heaviest part of the hill, with the hill leaning on it. I do not think it is only for the trees.

03 Â· Three Predictions

Okafor orchard, sixth terrace Â· Day Five, 16:20

Tomas Reyes unrolled sensor cable behind his truck like a man feeding out line to a very long, very patient animal. He was the authority's geotechnical engineer, a heavy, careful person who had spent a career being right about hills, and he was not offended by a sprinkler test. He was only unconvinced. Corinna stood by the tailgate with her arms folded and the date still faint on her hand.

Idowu Bello had walked over from the next plot with his hands in his pockets. He had cleared his half of the hillside in a dry year and sold it to the authority in the spring, and he looked at the bare slope the way a man looks at a decision.

Idowu BelloUse my ground. There is nothing on it to protect, and I would like to know whether I should be nervous.

Tomas wrote the predictions on the back of the authority's own form, so that no one could say later that they had been fitted to the result.

Tomas ReyesMine first. This hill is fractured sandstone. Water leaves through the cracks wherever you put it, so the orchard, your father's drain and Idowu's bare ground will all behave alike. The needles read pore pressure in kilopascals, and my model says the slope moves at ten. On the drain plot I set a twin needle six metres off the line, same stone, same depth. If I am right, the twins read alike.

Adaeze OkaforMine is shorter. Trees hold. The plots with trees stay quiet. Idowu's goes.

IlluminaraMine splits both of yours. The drain holds the hill. The needle on the drain line stays quiet, and its twin, six metres away in the same stone, does not. A plot with trees but no working drain goes with Idowu's ground.

Adaeze considered the slope, and Illuminara watched her decide to be generous.

Adaeze OkaforThe sixth terrace, east corner. The inlet silted the year of the big wind. Water stands there every spring, and I have never got a good guava out of it.

IlluminaraYou could have told me that on Monday.

Adaeze OkaforYou did not ask me a question on Monday. You told me a plan.

They settled the stakes without anyone raising a voice.

Adaeze OkaforIf Tomas is right, I sign the saddle cut myself. If I am right, you find another road. If my daughter is right, she says so to my face, and then she cleans my drain.

Corinna SalehOne condition from me. If the drain line crosses ten, the saddle is dead and I take the south route, sixteen months, and I tell the town today that the rains will find it unprotected. And if a test pour leaves under half a metre of freeboard, I tell them that day too.

Corinna SalehEvery day on this is a day the rains do not give back.

IlluminaraFour days. And Tomas has just written you a geotechnical report on the back of a form.

04 Â· The Needle

Okafor orchard, three plots Â· Day Nine, 13:05

The sprinklers were set to the storm the hill sees once in a century. Tomas stood over his needle with a flask of tea. Illuminara tipped a cup of green dye into the spring box and put the lid back, and Adaeze carried a bucket down to the drip and sat on it with her hand flat on the wall.

At thirty-eight minutes a green thread came out of the hole in the stone. Adaeze looked at it for a while.

Adaeze OkaforA kettle and a half.

The needle on the drain line never passed six kilopascals. Its twin, six metres off the line in the same stone at the same depth, climbed past nine. The corner of the sixth terrace, with its trees standing in it, crossed ten at ninety-five minutes, which was where Tomas had said the hill stopped being sure of itself. Idowu's bare ground crossed it at eighty-five. At a hundred and five a painted stake on Idowu's slope moved four millimetres, and Tomas shut off the water with a flat hand, like a man stopping an orchestra.

Tomas ReyesThe drain line was back to two forty minutes after the water stopped. Its twin was still above six. Fractured sandstone says those two match, and they do not. The corner went over ten with the trees in it. Bedrock is out. I was wrong, and I am glad to be wrong in a way I can write down.

Adaeze OkaforMy trees did not hold it.

Illuminara crouched beside her.

Adaeze OkaforDo not be kind. Be accurate.

IlluminaraYour trees did not hold the hill. Your father's drain did, and your trees have been drinking from it for eighty years. That is why the fruit is good.

Adaeze OkaforThat is kind and accurate. I will allow it.

Illuminara had drawn it in the truck cab on Day Seven, on the chance that she would be allowed to use it. She unrolled it on the bucket lid.

IlluminaraI cannot cut the third terrace and I will not drown your rows. So the canal goes in the bottom seven metres of the orchard, along the wall, in precast panels. The drip comes out into it through a one-way flap. In flood the canal cannot push water back up your father's drain, and in dry months the drain runs out into the canal as it always has. Tomas clears the silted inlet in the sixth corner. Where the flood outgrows the quarry, a weir at the head of the canal puts the surplus back in the river below the town.

Tomas ReyesFive metres of bed between concrete walls, two of water: ten square metres. Thirty-eight through ten is three point eight metres a second on a fall of one in four hundred. Panels take that. Soil would not, so no water touches the bank, and a metre of freeboard stands above the surface.

Corinna SalehAnd past thirty-eight?

Tomas ReyesThe weir hands it to the river below the town. That channel takes ten more before it tops its bank, and the worst flood in your survey peaks at forty-six.

Corinna SalehThen the proof comes before the last panel, not after it. A test pour at thirty-eight for six hours, Tomas's gauge on the rim. If it leaves under half a metre of freeboard, the strip is dead, Mrs Okafor's orchard is untouched, I take the south route, and I tell the town that day.

Tomas ReyesIt will not come near. I will stand at the gauge anyway.

Adaeze OkaforSeven metres of what? How many trees?

IlluminaraNine trees stand in the strip. I can save two, and you choose which.

Adaeze OkaforSeven, then, and I choose the seven. If the flood ever needs more than the strip, you ask me, and if I say no, it is no.

IlluminaraThe design works on the strip alone. I made it so it would still work if you refused me everything else.

Adaeze OkaforGood. And I plant the bank. Nobody else touches a root.

Adaeze OkaforYour flap is a door nobody can open. Every October my father poured the spring through the drain from the box above the third terrace and flushed it clean. Shut a flap on that and the silt stays in the pipe, and in five years you have my sixth-corner inlet, forty metres long.

Illuminara looked at the flap on her drawing. She had shown the water how to leave and had not shown anyone how to keep it leaving.

IlluminaraA chain. The flap lifts on a chain run up a sleeve to a hatch in the bank, and the flush goes through when you pull it.

Adaeze OkaforWhen I pull it. In October. And nobody else touches the chain.

Illuminara redrew the flap in pencil on the bucket lid, with her mother's hand on the corner to keep the paper flat.

Corinna SalehThe strip is not in the permit.

IlluminaraClause fourteen lets you vary the line by four hundred metres where the owner consents and a geotechnical report supports it. You have a consenting owner and a report on a form.

Corinna SalehSeventeen weeks to build. You have nineteen.

IlluminaraTwo weeks of slack.

Corinna SalehAnd rain does not read calendars.

05 Â· The Drip

Okafor orchard, new bank Â· Month Five, the first night of the rains

The last panel went in eight days before the rain. Along the lower wall stood seven pale stumps that Adaeze had chosen herself, oldest first, and had not allowed anyone to photograph. Tomas had run the test pour in month four, thirty-eight for six hours with his gauge on the rim, and the water had stood a metre and six centimetres below the lip. Corinna had signed the strip that afternoon.

The river began to rise at 01:40. Corinna's voice came out of the control hut radio, flat and exact, and at 02:05 she said the gates were open. The canal filled with a sound like a long breath let out. At the foot of the wall the flap on the drip clapped shut once under the pressure, and the thread of water stopped.

Adaeze stood under an umbrella that was not helping and took her daughter's arm without looking, with the grip of a woman checking a rope. Below them, Marrow Ford kept its lights on.

Corinna SalehBasin at half. Spill weir shut. Lower town is dry, every street.

Eight hundred and twenty thousand cubic metres went into the old quarry before the crest passed at a little after eight. It had room for eleven hundred thousand. The spill weir never opened.

When the canal began to drain, the flap lifted by itself. For a moment nothing happened, and then the stone under the wall began to tick. A chain ran from the flap up its sleeve to a hatch in the new bank, and the first October flush was already in Adaeze's planting plan, in her own hand.

Adaeze OkaforIt's running.

In the grey light Adaeze knelt on the new bank with a tray of rooted cuttings she had taken from the oldest of the seven before it came down. Illuminara held the pot while she settled the first one in.

Adaeze OkaforStraight, Nara. You still do not call before you come.

IlluminaraNo.

Adaeze OkaforGood. Then I will keep the kettle on.

THE QUICK PEOPLE OF THE PULSAR

Around the pulsar Wend-9, a plasma civilization that thinks a quarter of a million times faster than humans is walling off the hydrogen three hundred people need to breathe. Illuminara has fifteen days, a station that has been shouting a day-long sentence every six days of their time, and a rival physicist whose model fits the same numbers.

01 Â· Noise at Four Microseconds

Wend-9 Station, observation ring Â· Day One, 06:40

Three hundred people lived on Wend-9 Station, and every morning the torus around the pulsar let them have a little less of its hydrogen. The scoop on the station's leading edge had drunk eighty-one units a day for six years. It drank twenty-seven now, thirty-four percent of what it was built for, and the figure fell by three points with every morning.

Director Idris Maule laid it out beside the long window, where the plasma ring hung around a star too small to see. Hydrogen fed the fuel cells, the water, and the shuttle's one return burn.

Idris MauleReserve is twenty-two days at full draw, and the burn line is eight. At this rate I reach it on Day Sixteen. I sign the evacuation on Day Fifteen or I don't sign it. I would like to know what I am signing.

IlluminaraTell me about the noise.

Dr. Lenka Voss did not wait for the director.

Lenka VossIt is not a message, and I would put my name on that. The torus rings at point five two hertz. We range the orbit at point five oh. Drive a resonator near its mode and it rings back, and the field piles into a ridge where the ringing is strongest. The ridge is your shield. The replies are the ringdown, with a four-millisecond texture, because that is what plasma does.

Illuminara asked who had looked at the recordings and at what speed. Beatriz Okonjo, who ran communications and kept the baseband recorder because no one else remembered it, put up her hand like a schoolgirl.

Beatriz OkonjoThe summary the physicists read is sampled at one kilohertz. The recorder underneath takes ten million samples a second. It writes to cold storage and nobody opens it.

IlluminaraThen they have been listening to a book through a fan. Show me the raw.

The screen filled with spikes. Beatriz laid a ruler of faint lines across them and the spikes sat on it like swifts on a wire.

Beatriz OkonjoFour microseconds. Every burst lands on that grid, give or take a tenth. After each of our chirps there is one burst, a hundred and twenty-seven steps long.

IlluminaraOur chirp is a hundred and twenty-seven chips.

Lenka VossWhich smear to fifty milliseconds in the summary and look like ringing. A coincidence of code length is not a language.

IlluminaraNo. It is a rule I can fail. Half a millisecond is the length of a sneeze. If it is a sentence, it is one the station has been answering with a shout.

02 Â· The Ruler

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day One, 14:10

She wrote the arithmetic on the wardroom table in grease pencil so that nobody could say it had been done out of sight. She called it a ruler, not a fact, and drew one tick of theirs as one second of a human mind, to be thrown away the day it failed to predict anything.

IlluminaraThe station's ranging chirp lasts four tenths of a second. At a quarter of a million ticks per second, that is a hundred thousand ticks. By my ruler it is twenty-eight hours of unbroken pressure. Then one point six seconds of quiet, four and a half days. Then it starts again, every two seconds, five and three-quarter days, with the same bruise.

Ansel TarkovYou are asking me to believe that plasma has a grievance with our contract.

IlluminaraI am asking you to watch a ruler break. If it breaks, I will say so on this table. They answered every chirp, Mr. Tarkov. We answered their answers with the next chirp. To them we are a weather system that never stops and never listens.

Tarkov was the consortium's liaison, and the ranging data was his budget line. He folded his hands over it as if it might leave.

Lenka VossYour point five two is my resonance. You cannot take my number and call it your argument.

IlluminaraIt is the same number with two readings. You say it is how fast the torus rings. I say it is how long it takes for one thought to go once around the ring and come back as agreement, a council of a thousand voices that cannot decide anything shorter than one circuit. One point nine two seconds. We chirp at two. We interrupt them just before they finish every sentence they start.

Idris MauleAnd how do we tell which of you is right before Day Fifteen?

IlluminaraBy asking where you disagree, and going there first.

03 Â· Sealed Table

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day Two, 09:00

They built the table together, and it took three hours because Voss insisted on writing her own column and Illuminara insisted that hers be allowed to lose. Two hypotheses went down the left side. The tests ran across the top.

Lenka VossControl. We chirp at two seconds for six hours. Both of us say the shield narrows three points a day. If either of us says otherwise, the table is worthless.

IlluminaraAgreed. Second, a single chirp, then one second of quiet, then back to the cadence. Half a circuit. I say the gate cannot open and the closure continues. You say the ridge relaxes, two points of intake come back.

Lenka VossThird, one chirp and seven seconds of quiet. The torus relaxes smoothly, an e-fold in eleven seconds. A ramp. And the ringdown replays your code with the same sign, each pass half as large.

IlluminaraI say a gate. Nothing for three point eight four seconds, two full circuits from the end of the chirp, one to decide and one to act, and then the seam opens. Sharp, four tenths of a second wide, a chirp's width. And a reply that is not a replay. A resonator cannot run a code backwards. I predict the reverse and complement of whatever we send.

Beatriz OkonjoThen it has to be a code that nobody on this station has ever seen, and nobody can see before it flies.

She drew it from a strontium source in the lab, a count of decays in a window, a hundred and twenty-seven bits that existed nowhere until the counter ticked. Ansel Tarkov did not approve and did not stop her.

Ansel TarkovEvery second of quiet is a second of ranging lost. I want it recorded that I objected.

Idris MauleIt is recorded. The table is sealed. If the closure rate moves in the control, I want to hear it before I hear anything else.

Voss, Maule and Illuminara signed across the envelope flap, and Beatriz taped it to the wardroom wall where everyone could see that it had not moved.

04 Â· Seven Seconds of Quiet

Wend-9 Station, communications bay Â· Day Four, 11:12

The control had behaved. On Day Three the closure had held at its three points a day, in both columns. The one-second quiet had gone the way Illuminara had said and not the way Voss had said: the intake stayed where it was, and the ridge did not relax. Voss had looked at it for a long while and said nothing. Intake read twenty-two percent. The reserve stood at nineteen days.

Beatriz drew the code at 11:09. Maule stood behind her with the evacuation order folded in his pocket where he could feel its corners.

Beatriz OkonjoChirp in ten. Nine.

The ranging laser fired its four tenths of a second and the room went quiet in a way the station never was. For the first time in six years nobody sent anything at all.

Idris MauleOne second.

Lenka VossTorus is ringing down. Exactly as I said.

IlluminaraKeep counting.

Beatriz OkonjoTwo seconds. Three.

The ringdown on the main screen began a smooth slope and Voss's mouth set. Then the slope stopped, as if the torus had put a hand flat on a table.

Beatriz OkonjoThree point eight, three point eight three, three point eight four.

The seam opened. Hydrogen, which had been a thread, poured into the scoop, a rectangle of intake the width of exactly one chirp. At 11:12:04 it stood at ninety-four percent for four tenths of a second and then shut, with the neatness of a door closed by someone who had finished their sentence.

A burst sat in the record, half a millisecond long, laid on the four-microsecond grid. Beatriz ran the correlation against the code from the strontium counter.

Beatriz OkonjoIt's the code. Reversed. And complemented, every bit. Aligned to a fifth of a microsecond.

Lenka VossA linear system cannot do that. A resonator does not run backwards.

IlluminaraSay what would change your mind.

Lenka VossTen more codes. New ones, drawn by Beatriz, shown to no one. The gate at three point eight four each time, the reverse every time. If it fails once, I want the ringdown back.

IlluminaraYou will have all ten. And I would like to know, Dr. Voss, whether you are sorry.

Lenka VossI am the happiest woman on this station. I have been wrong in a direction no one has ever been wrong before.

05 Â· The Pause They Gave Back

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day Five, 08:30

The ten codes came back as the first one had, and by morning the seam was no longer a seam. The shield had come down to a low wall, and the intake read seventy-one percent and did not move. It was not what the station had been built to take. It was what the quick people had decided to give.

Idris MauleThe evacuation order is going in the shredder. And I am cutting the station's draw by almost a third, because they have told us what they will share. Seventy-one is a number I can live on. I would rather have a hundred.

IlluminaraThey did not offer a hundred. I would ask for nothing they have not offered.

Ansel Tarkov had been waiting for his turn and took it standing.

Ansel TarkovAnd my ranging? The consortium bought thirty solutions a minute. You are going to give them one.

IlluminaraA shorter chirp. Twenty milliseconds, once a minute. By my ruler that is eighty-three minutes of pressure once in a hundred and seventy days. The orbit solution loosens from forty metres to two hundred and twenty. Your contract tolerance is three hundred. You lose the tidal ripple you hoped to see. You keep the orbit.

Ansel TarkovI lose two years of a paper.

IlluminaraYou do. I cannot make that cost go away. You may refuse the new cadence, and I will tell the director so, and the seam may close again. I think you will not. I think you wanted to be the man who got the first dialogue with another kind of mind, not the man who lost it for a paper.

Tarkov looked at the grease-pencil table for a long time. He signed it on the condition that the agreement be reviewed in ninety days, and nobody argued with him.

At 09:40 they sent the first reply. Illuminara had Beatriz take the quick people's own burst, reverse it, complement it, and return it on the four-microsecond grid, the same courtesy handed back. Then Maule sat in front of a silent screen and counted.

Idris MauleThree point eight. Three point eight four.

The reply came with a tail on it. Forty steps that had not been in the code. The tail was identical on every exchange that followed, and no one on the station could say whether it was a name, a title or a shrug.

Beatriz found the other thing at noon. After each reply the quick people left a silence of one point nine two seconds, exactly one circuit of their ring, before the next possible word.

Beatriz OkonjoThey're waiting for us.

Lenka VossOf course they are. They are polite. It is the longest anyone has ever waited for anyone. For them it was five and a half days.

Maule stayed at the screen until the quiet ran out, as it did every day after that, and the station answered.

Lenka VossA linear resonator does not run backwards. That one is dead. A pumped one is another animal: a plasma mirror hands back the time-reverse of whatever hits it, and if its pump sits a half-turn out of phase, the complement comes free. I cannot rule that out from here.

IlluminaraThen convict it or acquit it in a number.

Lenka VossLag. A mirror builds its reply out of the pump, and builds slowly when the pump is weak. Starve it and it is late. Something with a clock answers at the clock. Ten new codes, drawn by Beatriz, shown to no one: five at full power, five at a quarter, two of those at half length. If the gate slides past seven seconds on the weak ones, it was my mirror, and I want the ringdown back.

IlluminaraYou will have all ten. And if it comes back late, I will say mirror at this table, in grease pencil.

Lenka VossI am the happiest woman on this station. Whichever of us loses, we are wrong in a direction no one has been wrong before.

05 Â· The Pause They Gave Back

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day Five, 08:30

The ten had come back in the night. The gate had stood at three point eight four seconds at full power, and at a quarter power, and at half length, give or take a hundredth. Nothing had been late. Voss read the lag column standing, then sat and wrote under her own heading, in pencil, mirror: late. It was not late.

Lenka VossThat is not a someone. It is a clock I did not put there. You may call it a someone.

IlluminaraI will call it the cheapest thing left.

The shield had come down to a low wall in the same hours, and the intake read seventy-one percent and did not move. It was not what the station had been built to take. It was what the quick people had decided to give.

Idris MauleFifty-seven units a day come in at seventy-one. The station can live on forty-eight. I am cutting our draw from eighty-one to fifty-six, which holds the reserve flat at nineteen days and leaves sixteen days over the burn line if the intake went to nothing tomorrow. The order comes back out if intake reads under forty-eight three days running, or the reserve falls under twelve. A fresh one takes eleven minutes to print.

Idris MauleBut I do not shred it on a gift whose terms we wrote ourselves. They have told us what they will share. Have they told us how often we may speak? Otherwise this is a schedule, and I know one when it arrives from the strong side of a table.

IlluminaraThen they write the terms. Two doors, written down before anyone fires. They may take one, bend one, or leave both. Door A is twenty milliseconds, once a minute. By my ruler that is eighty-three minutes of pressure once in a hundred and seventy-four days, and Mr. Tarkov's orbit solution loosens from forty metres to two hundred and twenty. Door B is the same twenty milliseconds every ten seconds: eighty-three minutes every twenty-nine days, and Mr. Tarkov keeps his orbit to sixty metres and his tidal ripple.

Ansel TarkovB. Obviously B.

IlluminaraB is six times the weather. It is the door that suits us, which is why none of us chooses. The strontium counter orders the blocks, six minutes each. A door is theirs when the gate stands at three point eight four on every chirp in its block and the intake does not move. A door is refused when a chirp goes unanswered and the intake steps down.

Idris MauleAnd if they take neither?

IlluminaraThen the order stays in your pocket, you sign on Day Fifteen, and I help you write it.

Tarkov took his turn standing.

Ansel TarkovAnd if they take B, I keep my paper?

IlluminaraYou keep your paper, and I will be a good deal more surprised than you. If they take A, you lose the ripple, and I cannot make that go away. You may refuse to fire A, and I will tell the director so. You wanted to be the man who got the first dialogue with another kind of mind, not the man who lost it for a paper.

Tarkov looked at the grease-pencil table for a long time, then signed it on the condition that both doors be offered again in ninety days.

The counter put B first. At 09:40 Beatriz fired twenty milliseconds, and Maule sat in front of a silent screen and counted.

Idris MauleThree point eight. Three point eight four.

The second chirp ten seconds later was answered on the same clock. The third was not. The seam stayed shut, the intake stepped from seventy-one to fifty-two and held there, and the room understood it had been told something without a word.

Idris MauleThat is a no.

IlluminaraThat is a door. Fire A.

In the second block the first chirp left the laser 0.7 seconds into a circuit of theirs, by Beatriz's clock, and the reply came with the seam shut and the tail doubled. The next chirp she slaved to the start of the circuit, to the half-hertz beat of the torus, and was answered, and so was the next, and so were all six. The intake did not move.

Beatriz OkonjoThey'll take A if we start where they start.

Lenka VossMy ring period. Naturally.

IlluminaraThey have told us where in the ring they begin to speak. The ruler does not have to choose between us, Dr. Voss. It is the same grid in both readings.

Maule fed the folded order into the shredder himself, slowly, and it came out a hundred strips of identical width.

Idris MauleSeventy-one is a number I can live on. I would rather have a hundred.

IlluminaraThey did not offer a hundred. I would ask for nothing they have not offered.

Ansel TarkovI lose two years of a paper.

IlluminaraYou do.

Every reply came with a tail on it, forty steps that had not been in the code. The tail was identical on every exchange that followed, and no one on the station could say whether it was a name, a title or a shrug.

Beatriz found the other thing at noon. After each reply the quick people left a silence of one point nine two seconds, one circuit of their ring, before the next possible word.

Beatriz OkonjoThey're waiting for us.

Lenka VossOf course they are. They are polite. It is the longest anyone has ever waited for anyone. For them it was five and a half days.

Maule stayed at the screen until the quiet ran out, as it did every day after that, and the station answered.

THE QUICK PEOPLE OF THE PULSAR

Around the pulsar Wend-9, a plasma civilization that thinks a quarter of a million times faster than humans is walling off the hydrogen three hundred people need to breathe. Illuminara has fifteen days, a station that has been shouting a day-long sentence every six days of their time, and a rival physicist whose model fits the same numbers.

01 Â· Noise at Four Microseconds

Wend-9 Station, observation ring Â· Day One, 06:40

Three hundred people lived on Wend-9 Station, and every morning the torus around the pulsar let them have a little less of its hydrogen. The scoop on the station's leading edge had drunk eighty-one units a day for six years. It drank twenty-seven now, thirty-four percent of what it was built for, and the figure fell by three points with every morning.

Director Idris Maule laid it out beside the long window, where the plasma ring hung around a star too small to see. Hydrogen fed the fuel cells, the water, and the shuttle's one return burn.

Idris MauleReserve is twenty-two days at full draw, and the burn line is eight. At this rate I reach it on Day Sixteen. I sign the evacuation on Day Fifteen or I don't sign it. I would like to know what I am signing.

IlluminaraTell me about the noise.

Dr. Lenka Voss did not wait for the director.

Lenka VossIt is not a message, and I would put my name on that. The torus rings at point five two hertz. We range the orbit at point five oh. Drive a resonator near its mode and it rings back, and the field piles into a ridge where the ringing is strongest. The ridge is your shield. The replies are the ringdown, with a four-millisecond texture, because that is what plasma does.

Illuminara asked who had looked at the recordings and at what speed. Beatriz Okonjo, who ran communications and kept the baseband recorder because no one else remembered it, put up her hand like a schoolgirl.

Beatriz OkonjoThe summary the physicists read is sampled at one kilohertz. The recorder underneath takes ten million samples a second. It writes to cold storage and nobody opens it.

IlluminaraThen they have been listening to a book through a fan. Show me the raw.

The screen filled with spikes. Beatriz laid a ruler of faint lines across them and the spikes sat on it like swifts on a wire.

Beatriz OkonjoFour microseconds. Every burst lands on that grid, give or take a tenth. After each of our chirps there is one burst, a hundred and twenty-seven steps long.

IlluminaraOur chirp is a hundred and twenty-seven chips.

Lenka VossWhich smear to fifty milliseconds in the summary and look like ringing. A coincidence of code length is not a language.

IlluminaraNo. It is a rule I can fail. Half a millisecond is the length of a sneeze. If it is a sentence, it is one the station has been answering with a shout.

02 Â· The Ruler

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day One, 14:10

She wrote the arithmetic on the wardroom table in grease pencil so that nobody could say it had been done out of sight. She called it a ruler, not a fact, and drew one tick of theirs as one second of a human mind, to be thrown away the day it failed to predict anything.

IlluminaraThe station's ranging chirp lasts four tenths of a second. At a quarter of a million ticks per second, that is a hundred thousand ticks. By my ruler it is twenty-eight hours of unbroken pressure. Then one point six seconds of quiet, four and a half days. Then it starts again, every two seconds, five and three-quarter days, with the same bruise.

Ansel TarkovYou are asking me to believe that plasma has a grievance with our contract.

IlluminaraI am asking you to watch a ruler break. If it breaks, I will say so on this table. They answered every chirp, Mr. Tarkov, and we answered their answers with the next chirp. To them we are a weather system that never stops and never listens.

Tarkov was the consortium's liaison, and the ranging data was his budget line. He folded his hands over it as if it might leave.

Lenka VossYour point five two is my resonance. You cannot take my number and call it your argument.

IlluminaraIt is the same number with two readings. You say it is how fast the torus rings. I say it is how long it takes for one thought to go once around the ring and come back as agreement, a council of a thousand voices that cannot decide anything shorter than one circuit. One point nine two seconds. We chirp at two. We interrupt them just before they finish every sentence they start.

Idris MauleAnd how do we tell which of you is right before Day Fifteen?

IlluminaraBy asking where you disagree, and going there first.

03 Â· Sealed Table

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day Two, 09:00

They built the table together, and it took three hours because Voss insisted on writing her own column and Illuminara insisted that hers be allowed to lose. Two hypotheses went down the left side. The tests ran across the top.

Lenka VossControl. We chirp at two seconds for six hours. Both of us say the shield narrows three points a day. If either of us says otherwise, the table is worthless.

IlluminaraAgreed. Second, a single chirp, then one second of quiet, then back to the cadence. Half a circuit. I say the gate cannot open and the closure continues. You say the ridge relaxes, two points of intake come back.

Lenka VossThird, one chirp and seven seconds of quiet. The torus relaxes smoothly, an e-fold in eleven seconds. A ramp. And the ringdown replays your code with the same sign, each pass half as large.

IlluminaraI say a gate. Nothing for three point eight four seconds, two full circuits from the end of the chirp, one to decide and one to act, and then the seam opens. Sharp, four tenths of a second wide, a chirp's width. And a reply that is not a replay. A resonator cannot run a code backwards. I predict the reverse and complement of whatever we send.

Beatriz OkonjoThen it has to be a code that nobody on this station has ever seen, and nobody can see before it flies.

She drew it from a strontium source in the lab, a count of decays in a window, a hundred and twenty-seven bits that existed nowhere until the counter ticked. Ansel Tarkov did not approve and did not stop her.

Ansel TarkovEvery second of quiet is a second of ranging lost. I want it recorded that I objected.

Idris MauleIt is recorded. The table is sealed. If the closure rate moves in the control, I want to hear it before I hear anything else.

Voss, Maule and Illuminara signed across the envelope flap, and Beatriz taped it to the wardroom wall where everyone could see that it had not moved.

04 Â· Seven Seconds of Quiet

Wend-9 Station, communications bay Â· Day Four, 11:12

The control had behaved. On Day Three the closure had held at its three points a day, in both columns. The one-second quiet had gone the way Illuminara had said and not the way Voss had said: the intake stayed where it was, and the ridge did not relax. Voss had looked at it a long while and said nothing. Intake read twenty-two percent. The reserve stood at nineteen days.

Beatriz drew the code at 11:09. Maule stood behind her with the evacuation order folded in his pocket where he could feel its corners.

Beatriz OkonjoChirp in ten. Nine.

The ranging laser fired its four tenths of a second and the room went quiet in a way the station never was. For the first time in six years nobody sent anything at all.

Idris MauleOne second.

Lenka VossTorus is ringing down. Exactly as I said.

IlluminaraKeep counting.

Beatriz OkonjoTwo seconds. Three.

The ringdown on the main screen began a smooth slope and Voss's mouth set. Then the slope stopped, as if the torus had put a hand flat on a table.

Beatriz OkonjoThree point eight, three point eight three, three point eight four.

The seam opened. Hydrogen, which had been a thread, poured into the scoop, a rectangle of intake the width of exactly one chirp. At 11:12:04 it stood at ninety-four percent for four tenths of a second and then shut, with the neatness of a door closed by someone who had finished their sentence.

A burst sat in the record, half a millisecond long, laid on the four-microsecond grid. Beatriz ran the correlation against the code from the strontium counter.

Beatriz OkonjoIt's the code. Reversed. And complemented, every bit. Aligned to a fifth of a microsecond.

Lenka VossA linear resonator does not run backwards. That one is dead. A pumped one is another animal: a plasma mirror hands back the time-reverse of whatever hits it, and if its pump sits a half-turn out of phase, the complement comes free. I cannot rule that out from here.

IlluminaraThen convict it or acquit it in a number.

Lenka VossLag. A mirror builds its reply out of the pump, and builds slowly when the pump is weak. Starve it and it is late. Something with a clock answers at the clock. Ten new codes, drawn by Beatriz, shown to no one: five at full power, five at a quarter, two of those at half length. If the gate slides past seven seconds on the weak ones, it was my mirror, and I want the ringdown back.

IlluminaraYou will have all ten. And if it comes back late, I will say mirror at this table, in grease pencil.

Lenka VossI am the happiest woman on this station. Whichever of us loses, we are wrong in a direction no one has been wrong before.

05 Â· The Pause They Gave Back

Wend-9 Station, wardroom Â· Day Five, 08:30

The ten had come back in the night. The gate had stood at three point eight four seconds at full power, and at a quarter power, and at half length, give or take a hundredth. Nothing had been late. Voss read the lag column standing, then sat and wrote under her own heading, in pencil, mirror: late. It was not late.

Lenka VossThat is not a someone. It is a clock I did not put there. You may call it a someone.

IlluminaraI will call it the cheapest thing left.

The shield had come down to a low wall in the same hours, and the intake read seventy-one percent and did not move. It was not what the station had been built to take. It was what the quick people had decided to give.

Idris MauleFifty-seven units a day come in at seventy-one. The station can live on forty-eight. I am cutting our draw from eighty-one to fifty-six, which holds the reserve flat at nineteen days, fifteen hundred and thirty-nine units. The burn line is six hundred and forty-eight. If the intake went to nothing tomorrow, that leaves eight hundred and ninety-one units above the line, sixteen days at fifty-six. The order comes back out if intake reads under forty-eight three days running, or the reserve falls under twelve days. A fresh one takes eleven minutes to print.

Idris MauleBut I do not shred it on a gift whose terms we wrote ourselves. They have told us what they will share. Have they told us how often we may speak? Otherwise this is a schedule, and I know one when it arrives from the strong side of a table.

IlluminaraThen they write the terms. Two doors, written down before anyone fires. They may take one, bend one, or leave both. Door A is twenty milliseconds, once a minute. By my ruler that is eighty-three minutes of pressure once in a hundred and seventy-four days, and Mr. Tarkov's orbit solution loosens from forty metres to two hundred and twenty. Door B is the same twenty milliseconds every ten seconds: eighty-three minutes every twenty-nine days, and Mr. Tarkov keeps his orbit to sixty metres and his tidal ripple.

Ansel TarkovB. Obviously B.

IlluminaraB is six times the weather. It is the door that suits us, which is why none of us chooses. The strontium counter orders the blocks, six minutes each. A door is theirs when the gate stands at three point eight four on every chirp in its block and the intake does not step down. A door is refused when a chirp goes unanswered and the intake steps down.

Idris MauleAnd if they take neither?

IlluminaraThen the order stays in your pocket, you sign on Day Fifteen, and I help you write it.

Tarkov took his turn standing.

Ansel TarkovAnd if they take B, I keep my paper?

IlluminaraYou keep your paper, and I will be a good deal more surprised than you. If they take A, you lose the ripple, and I cannot make that go away. You may refuse to fire A, and I will tell the director so. You wanted to be the man who got the first dialogue with another kind of mind, not the man who lost it for a paper.

Tarkov looked at the grease-pencil table for a long time, then signed it on the condition that both doors be offered again in ninety days.

The counter put B first. At 09:40 Beatriz fired twenty milliseconds, and Maule sat in front of a silent screen and counted.

Idris MauleThree point eight. Three point eight four.

The second chirp ten seconds later was answered on the same clock. The third was not. The seam stayed shut, the intake stepped from seventy-one to fifty-two and held there, and the room understood it had been told something without a word.

Fifty-two was forty-two units against a draw of fifty-six. Maule wrote the fourteen short in the margin of the order; the eight hundred and ninety-one above the line would carry that for sixty days. He did not look up from it.

Idris MauleThat is a no.

IlluminaraThat is a door. Fire A.

In the second block the first chirp left the laser 0.7 seconds into a circuit of theirs, by Beatriz's clock, and the reply came with the seam shut and the tail doubled. The intake did not step down further. The next chirp she slaved to the start of the circuit, to the half-hertz beat of the torus, and was answered, and so was the next, and so were all six. From the second answer the intake climbed from fifty-two, and when the block ended it read seventy-one again and stayed there.

Beatriz OkonjoThey'll take A if we start where they start.

Lenka VossMy ring period. Naturally.

IlluminaraThey have told us where in the ring they begin to speak. The ruler does not have to choose between us, Dr. Voss. It is the same grid in both readings.

Maule watched the screen read seventy-one for a full quarter of an hour. Then he fed the folded order into the shredder himself, slowly, and it came out a hundred strips of identical width.

Idris MauleSeventy-one is a number I can live on. I would rather have a hundred.

IlluminaraThey did not offer a hundred. I would ask for nothing they have not offered.

Ansel TarkovI lose two years of a paper.

IlluminaraYou do.

Every reply came with a tail on it, forty steps that had not been in the code. The tail was identical on every exchange that followed, and no one on the station could say whether it was a name, a title or a shrug.

Beatriz found the other thing at noon. After each reply the quick people left a silence of one point nine two seconds, one circuit of their ring, before the next possible word.

Beatriz OkonjoThey're waiting for us.

Lenka VossOf course they are. They are polite. It is the longest anyone has ever waited for anyone. For them it was five and a half days.

Maule stayed at the screen until the quiet ran out, as it did every day after that, and the station answered.

THE WORD THAT HAD NO SECOND SPEAKER

A three-hundred-year-old treaty turns on one word that a mining company reads as lease and a valley reads as lend, and only one living person speaks the language. Illuminara has nine days, a court that wants a translator, and a witness who will not be made into a key.

01 Â· Eighty-nine

Kessel Reach tribunal, hearing room two Â· Day One, 08:10

The Compact of the Seven Springs ran to four pages, and the whole of it turned on a single word of the IrÃº language. Harrow & Vane Minerals read the word as lease. The people of the IrÃº valley read it as lend. Between the two readings lay a ridge, the aquifer under the ridge, and seven thousand people who drank from it.

The word was oruma. The tribunal had nine days to say what it meant, and no dictionary could help it. The last IrÃº school had burned with its books, and the last person who spoke the language with the weather and the work still in it sat in the front row under a grey blanket. Ilo Marrow was eighty-nine. He had been asked to testify six times and had refused six times.

Pell Ardent, the courtâ€™s translator, stood by the bench with a folder of field recordings and the look of a woman who had not slept. She spoke to Illuminara without turning her head.

Pell ArdentCounsel for the company telephoned me at home last night. She said a reading of lease would be very much in keeping with the recordings. I said I would read what the recordings said. She said she was sure I would.

IlluminaraDid you hear a threat?

Pell ArdentI heard someone checking how much weight I could carry. If I am the only voice, I will be the weight.

Illuminara had once been paid to carry words across a courtroom without letting them change on the way. She knew what the bench wanted from her, and it was a second voice. One speaker could be doubted. Two would be a record. There was no second.

She went down the aisle and knelt by Iloâ€™s chair so that he would not have to look up.

IlluminaraI can interpret for you. I have done it in courts. I would not change a word.

Ilo MarrowYou are kind, and you would make me a quotation. I would speak, you would speak, and the court would keep yours.

IlluminaraThen I will not ask what the word means.

His eyebrows rose a fingerâ€™s width.

IlluminaraI will ask what a person does after hearing it.

Ilo looked at his hands for a long time, spotted and knuckled, the hands of a man who had lent a great many things.

Ilo MarrowThat is a question for a seed. I will speak to objects, girl. I will not speak to you about them.

Wren Odira, who had kept the courtâ€™s clock since the first morning because nobody else would, wrote 08:41 in her notebook and beside it, objects only.

02 Â· What a Person Does Next

Three hamlets of the IrÃº valley Â· Days Two to Four

The language was nearly gone, but the practice had not died with it. Illuminara walked to three people who lived a dayâ€™s walk apart, had never compared notes, and spoke only the common tongue: Teodora Brisk at Upper Haddow, whose cress and barley trays went from house to house each spring; Hamid Sorel, warden of the reservoir boats; and Anke Doyle, who lent the names of dead grandmothers to newborns and took them back. Wren drove the borrowed truck and wrote down where each of them stood.

She asked each the same question in the same words. What does a person do after you say oruma?

Teodora BriskYou take the measure in March. In May you bring it back with a handful over. And you say what failed.

Hamid SorelThe boat goes out leaking. It comes home caulked, with your name cut in the new plank. Nobody hands back a boat the way they found it. That is how I know they used it.

Anke DoyleThe name comes back with a line on the board. What the child did with it.

Three trades, three ways of returning, one shape: use, change, account. Illuminara set the shape aside as a hypothesis, not an answer, and asked each of them the question that could break it.

IlluminaraWhat if the borrower hands it back exactly as found?

Teodora, Hamid and Anke made the same small face, three days and thirty miles apart.

Teodora BriskThen they are telling me they never touched it. Or that they hid what they did.

Illuminara wrote that down and then went looking for her best enemy. Ines Calder, counsel for the company, had put one document into evidence that she believed nobody could answer: oruma of the reservoir ferry right to the Crowe family in 1794, never called back in two hundred and twelve years, and no object ever returned.

Hamid SorelThe log came back. Every winter the Crowes carried the ferry log up to the council hall. That is what coming back looks like when the thing is a river.

Wren OdiraIt fits the companyâ€™s second reading if you squint. Keep the thing, send a token. You told me to say when something fits.

IlluminaraIt fits mine if you do not squint. That does not make it proof. It makes it the place where the test must be able to fail.

03 Â· Three Columns

Tribunal annex Â· Day Five, 07:30

Illuminara spread one sheet across the annex table and drew two columns before anyone could object to the first. LEASE: a fixed term, a fee, the thing returned as found. LEND: use, then the thing returned changed, with an account, and the lender free to call it back. Under each she wrote what a borrower would do with a measure of seed, a skiff and a name-board when the season ended.

Ines CalderPerforming a gloss in a courtroom shows what the performers believe. I will oppose it.

IlluminaraSo the performer will be your surveyor, who has never heard the word spoken by anyone who meant it. And the judges will be three keepers behind a screen who will not know which protocol they are watching. Correct the lease column if I have drawn it unfairly.

Calder read the lease column slowly. She struck one line, corrected another, and then, unprompted, drew a third column and headed it GRANT: the thing kept, a token sent home each year. It was her fallback, and she wrote it down where the tribunal could see that she had one.

Maren Tolliver, the companyâ€™s surveyor, who had walked Ridge Nine for eleven years and was said to know the aquifer better than the hydrologists did, agreed to do the handing back. She asked one thing.

Maren TolliverWho decides whether I did it right?

IlluminaraNot Ilo. If this rests on one old man, it is a quotation again. Each keeper decides for the thing they keep. If a keeper cannot decide, that handing back counts as unresolved, and it does not count for me.

Wren OdiraAnd if he will not say anything to an object?

IlluminaraThen the record says he refused. A refusal is not an answer, and I will not turn it into one.

The nine handings back, three protocols on three objects, would be drawn from a hat. The envelope held three predictions that mattered. The keepers would call LEND closed. They would call LEASE not closed. And GRANT they would push back across the table, because a thing kept is a sale. Wren signed across the flap at 07:52.

04 Â· The Surveyorâ€™s Hands

Upper Haddow seed house Â· Day Six, 10:00

A linen screen divided the seed house, with a slot cut low in it that showed only hands and the thing in them. On one side sat Teodora, Hamid and Anke at separate tables, each with a pencil and a card marked CLOSED and NOT CLOSED. On the other stood Maren Tolliver with her sleeves rolled, and Ilo Marrow in a chair beside a measure of barley seed, a skiff with a weeping seam and a pine name-board scored with nine names.

Each time, Ilo spoke a short phrase to the object in IrÃº, not to the woman, and each time Illuminara wrote down only the time. She did not translate. Tolliver did what the sheet in her hand said.

Under the lease protocol she paid the fee and handed the seed back exactly as found, measure for measure. Teodora looked at it for a long moment and turned her card to NOT CLOSED. Under the lend protocol Tolliver had run a germination test on the ridge soil with the seed, and she returned the measure with a handful over and a page of what had rotted. Teodora turned her card to CLOSED and pushed the measure to the middle of her table, where a lender sets down what she has received.

The skiff went the same way. As found, the seam still wept, and Hamid ran a thumb along it.

Hamid SorelLeaking. Exactly as it was. She never got in it.

He marked NOT CLOSED. Under lend she had caulked the seam in an hour and cut her name in the plank, and he marked CLOSED. Under grant she kept the skiff and sent home one nail, and Hamid pushed the nail back through the slot with a forefinger. Ask the price, the gesture said, and the rule that had been written the day before scored it NOT CLOSED.

At the name-board, under the lend protocol, Tolliver cut a line of account into the pine. Ilo spoke to the board for longer than the formula needed, quietly, in a voice that was not for the room. Illuminara wrote untranslated, Ilo to the board and nothing else.

Wren OdiraWhat did he say?

IlluminaraA man spoke to a board. I will not make it into something he said to me.

By one oâ€™clock the envelope was open. All nine of the keepersâ€™ calls on their own objects matched the prediction. Of the twenty-seven calls the three keepers made together, twenty-five matched. The two that did not were Teodora and Hamid, who had both marked the name-board GRANT as CLOSED, reasoning that a single token line was a faint account. Anke, who owned the board, had marked it NOT CLOSED and drawn a small question mark beside it. Illuminara entered the handing back as unresolved and did not smooth it.

Calder had watched the lease handing back all morning with her arms folded.

Maren TolliverI did not think of doing it any other way than as found. In my trade that is clean. In theirs it is a lie.

Ines CalderYou were asked to perform a word you do not speak.

Maren TolliverI was asked what a decent person does with a borrowed thing. Three people who have never met told me. I did not need IrÃº for that.

05 Â· The Dry Shoulder

Kessel Reach tribunal, hearing room two Â· Day Nine, 15:20

Judge Anselm Rhee read for forty minutes. He said the tribunal did not hold that a demonstration was a dictionary, and that Ilo Marrowâ€™s sentence to the board would stay in the record as untranslated. He found, on the evidence of practice from three independent keepers, a prediction sealed before the test, a hostile reading allowed to fail, and one surveyorâ€™s honest hands, that oruma obliged an account and a return changed by use, and carried the lenderâ€™s right of recall. No account could be given of a mine above the aquifer that returned the springs unharmed. Harrow & Vaneâ€™s claim to Ridge Nine reverted to the valley.

He then read the councilâ€™s offer, made in open session on Day Seven: the Dry Shoulder, a bare saddle of rock south of the ridge, with ore a third thinner, lent to the company on the same word and on the same terms of account.

Ines CalderI have advised my client not to appeal. Our own surveyorâ€™s hands are in the record. We ask only that the account be annual, and that recall be written out, so that no one on either side has to guess what the word means in a bad year.

It was an expensive sentence for her to say. At the Dry Shoulder four hundred mining jobs would never exist. Oren Basque, the council speaker, who had wanted the whole ridge and the company gone from it, signed with a hand that shook. The Vesper and Hale families lost the summer pasture on the saddle and left the hall without speaking to anyone.

The tribunalâ€™s last order directed the company to fund a school. Ilo Marrow answered it from his chair, in the common tongue, before the judge had finished.

Ilo MarrowI will not be paid for it. I will lend it. Nine people, and each of them takes one thing, a seed or a boat or a name, and brings it back changed.

Pell Ardent read the order into the record without a folder. Maren Tolliver asked, from the back of the hall, whether she might be counted among the nine, and nobody laughed.

At 16:04, on the courthouse steps, Ilo put a measure of seed into the hands of the youngest of them, a girl of seven called Pia, and said the word to her, not to the seed. Pia answered in IrÃº, loudly and wrongly, and the old man laughed until he coughed.

Wren OdiraThat is a second speaker.

IlluminaraThat is a first one. He needs nine more, and then they need each other.

Wren wrote the time. The word had had one speaker for a long season, and by spring it would have ten.

Skiff, oruma to Aldous Sorel for the fishing, the plank to be cut and the boat to come home. Called home in the flood of 1731. It came.

She laid Clause Four beside it. The same frame stood in both: the word, a holder, a use, and a clause saying what must happen at the end. Hamid SorelThe Crowes carry the ferry log up to the council hall every winter. That is what coming back looks like when the thing is a river.

03 Â· Three Columns

Tribunal annex Â· Day Four, 07:30

Illuminara spread one sheet across the annex table and drew two columns before anyone could object to the first.
