IlluminaraWrite your best reading. Not the one that is easy to beat. The one a careful lawyer would defend in her sleep.

Calder took the pen. She did not write the lease Illuminara had expected, a fee and the thing returned as found.

Ines CalderA lease allows wear. The tenant maintains, at her own cost, and keeps a written account of the days she used it. The thing comes back sound, and the lessor does not take it back before the term runs, for any reason, because that is what a term is.

She headed the column LEASE and underlined any reason.

IlluminaraYour column and mine agree on the seed, the skiff and the name-board. A tenant mends the seam and cuts a line on the board too.

Ines CalderThen your objects cannot tell us anything.

IlluminaraThey can tell us whether the keepers recognize their own rules. They cannot tell us where we part, and we part in one place. Can the lender call it home before the term, if she has to?

Calder was silent for a breath. Then she did what Illuminara had not asked, and chose the case.

Ines CalderA sluice paddle from the reservoir office, lent for the term of the hearing. Mid-term, the lender calls it back. Twice: once because the water is rising, once for no reason at all. And the judge is not one of your three keepers, who have heard your table. Brann Crowe. His family has held the ferry for two hundred years, and he has no reason to love you.

Maren Tolliver, the companyâ€™s surveyor, who had walked Ridge Nine for eleven years, agreed to do the handing back. She asked one thing.

IlluminaraNot Ilo. If this rests on one old man, it is a quotation again. Each keeper decides for the thing they keep, and Mr Crowe the paddle. If a judge cannot decide, it counts as unresolved, and it does not count for me.

05 Â· The Dry Shoulder

Kessel Reach tribunal, hearing room two Â· Day Nine, 15:20

On Day Seven the council had offered the company the Dry Shoulder, a bare saddle of rock south of the ridge, with ore a third thinner, lent on the same word and on the same terms of account. THE WORD THAT HAD NO SECOND SPEAKER

A three-hundred-year-old treaty turns on one word that a mining company reads as lease and a valley reads as lend, and only one living person speaks the language. Illuminara has nine days, a court that wants a translator, and a witness who will not be made into a key.

01 Â· Eighty-nine

Kessel Reach tribunal, hearing room two Â· Day One, 08:10

The Compact of the Seven Springs ran to four pages, and the whole of it turned on a single word of the IrÃº language. Clause Four gave Ridge Nine oruma to Harrow & Vane Minerals for the winning of ore, the springs to be rendered whole. The company read the word as lease. The people of the IrÃº valley read it as lend. Between the two readings lay a ridge, the aquifer under the ridge, and seven thousand people who drank from it.

The tribunal had nine days; the companyâ€™s interim licence to blast expired at 08:00 on the tenth. No dictionary could help. The last IrÃº school had burned with its books, and the last person who spoke the language with the work still in it sat in the front row under a grey blanket. Ilo Marrow was eighty-nine. He had been asked to testify six times and had refused six times.

Pell Ardent, the courtâ€™s translator, stood by the bench with a folder of field recordings and the look of a woman who had not slept.

Pell ArdentCounsel for the company telephoned me at home last night. She said a reading of lease would be very much in keeping with the recordings. I said I would read what they said. She said she was sure I would.

IlluminaraDid you hear a threat?

Pell ArdentI heard someone checking how much weight I could carry. If I am the only voice, I will be the weight.

She knew what the bench wanted from her, and it was a second voice. One speaker could be doubted. Two would be a record. There was no second.

She went down the aisle and knelt by Iloâ€™s chair so that he would not have to look up.

IlluminaraI can interpret for you. I have done it in courts. I would not change a word.

Ilo MarrowYou are kind, and you would make me a quotation. I would speak, you would speak, and the court would keep yours.

IlluminaraThen I will not ask what the word means.

His eyebrows rose a fingerâ€™s width.

IlluminaraI will ask what a person does after hearing it.

Ilo looked at his hands for a long time, the hands of a man who had lent a great many things.

Ilo MarrowThat is a question for a seed. I will speak to objects, girl. I will not speak to you about them.

Wren Odira, who kept the courtâ€™s clock because nobody else would, wrote 08:41 in her notebook and beside it, objects only. Under it she ruled a line and wrote 216 h from 08:00.

02 Â· What a Person Does Next

Three hamlets of the IrÃº valley Â· Days Two and Three

The language was nearly gone, but the practice had not died with it. Illuminara went to three people who lived a dayâ€™s walk apart, had never compared notes, and spoke only the common tongue: Teodora Brisk at Upper Haddow, whose cress and barley trays went from house to house each spring; Hamid Sorel, warden of the reservoir boats; and Anke Doyle, who lent the names of dead grandmothers to newborns and took them back.

She asked each the same question in the same words. What does a person do after you say oruma?

Teodora BriskYou take the measure in March. In May you bring it back with a handful over. And you say what failed.

Hamid SorelThe boat goes out leaking. It comes home caulked, with your name cut in the new plank. Nobody hands back a boat the way they found it. That is how I know they used it.

Anke DoyleThe name comes back with a line on the board. What the child did with it.

Use, change, account. Illuminara set the shape aside as a hypothesis. These three had described the practice her columns were drawn from, so they could not also be the people who proved the columns right.

At Hamidâ€™s desk she stopped him turning a page. His boat-book, Exhibit Fourteen, had been logged as a repair ledger and nobody had read it for a verb. The oldest entry was 1727, a year after the Compact.

Skiff, oruma to Aldous Sorel for the fishing, the plank to be cut and the boat to come home. Called home in the flood of 1731. It came.

She laid Clause Four beside it. The same frame stood in both: the word, a holder, a use, and a clause saying what must happen at the end. The ferry grant Ines Calder had put in evidence herself, oruma to the Crowes for the crossing, the log to be borne up each winter, carried it too. It had never been called back in two hundred and twelve years.

Wren OdiraIt fits the companyâ€™s second reading if you squint. Keep the thing, send a token. You told me to say when something fits.

IlluminaraIt fits mine if you do not squint. That does not make it proof. The frame shows the word came with an ending-clause. It does not show that the clause was a debt, or that anyone could call it early. For that we need a case nobody on my side has built.

Hamid SorelThe Crowes carry the ferry log up to the council hall every winter. That is what coming back looks like when the thing is a river.

03 Â· Three Columns

Tribunal annex Â· Day Four, 07:30

Illuminara spread one sheet across the annex table and drew two columns. LEND: use, then the thing returned changed, with an account, and the lender free to call it back. GRANT: the thing kept, a token sent home each year. She left the third column blank and turned the sheet to face Ines Calder.

IlluminaraWrite your best reading. Not the one that is easy to beat. The one a careful lawyer would defend in her sleep.

Calder took the pen. She did not write the lease Illuminara had expected.

Ines CalderA lease allows wear. The tenant maintains, at her own cost, and keeps a written account of the days she used it. The thing comes back sound, and the lessor does not take it back before the term runs, for any reason, because that is what a term is. The springs are rendered whole when the term ends, and the account says how.

She headed the column LEASE and underlined any reason.

IlluminaraYour column and mine agree on the seed, the skiff and the name-board. A tenant mends the seam and cuts a line on the board too.

Ines CalderThen your objects cannot tell us anything.

IlluminaraThey can tell us whether the keepers recognize their own rules. They cannot tell us where we part, and we part in one place. Can the lender call it home before the term, if she has to?

Calder was silent for a breath. Then she did what Illuminara had not asked, and chose the case.

Ines CalderA sluice paddle from the reservoir office, lent for the term of the hearing. Mid-term, the lender calls it back. Twice: once because the water is rising, once for no reason at all. And the judge is not one of your three keepers, who have heard your table. Brann Crowe. His family has held the ferry for two hundred years, and he has no reason to love you.

IlluminaraNot Ilo. If this rests on one old man, it is a quotation again. Each keeper decides for the thing they keep, and Mr Crowe the paddle. If a judge cannot decide, it counts as unresolved, and it does not count for me.

At 09:15 Tolliver took the objects. Wren logged the minute she sowed two trays of cress, ridge soil and valley soil. Forty-eight hours, said Teodora, was a cress season.

THE LIBRARY AT THE EDGE OF NOISE

A library hidden in the hiss between galaxies answers any question perfectly and takes one memory from whoever asks. With a famine closing on three worlds, Illuminara has to learn what the Library is really charging for, and whether anyone has to pay it with themselves.

01 Â· The Price of an Answer

Survey vessel Tamsin Reach, three million light-years beyond the Calder Rim Â· ship-day 211, 06:10

The Library had never spoken first. It lay in the hiss between galaxies, in what every instrument on the Tamsin Reach insisted was noise, and the crew could read it only by asking. Ask a question and the noise folded itself into a complete answer, exact to the last digit. A moment later the person who had asked reached for something and found it gone.

Captain Hadiza Moreau had lost the name of her first ship. Navigator Soren Eklund could still see the photographs of his wife, but they no longer meant her. Bartosz Wende, the linguist, had asked for bread at supper and found that Lowvale, the language his grandmother had spoken to him, had left him between one sentence and the next.

Illuminara stood at the question console with her hands behind her back. Nobody had told her not to touch it. She had simply understood, on arriving, that the console was the only dangerous object aboard.

HadizaEleven questions, five of us paid. We asked it about the Calder famine in pieces, because the whole is too large to ask. The pieces are right. They do not add up. The relief fleet is six hundred ships, and every correction we send makes the next shortage worse. Tarn runs dry in forty days, Veyra in sixty, and Ossel after that. Forty million people. The model needs one answer we cannot compute, and the only place that answer lives is behind that console.

BartoszAnd the price is us. Tell her, Hadiza. Tell her what it takes.

IlluminaraI would rather you told me what it took. Exactly. Not what it cost you.

They gave her the list, slowly, in the flat voices of people reading an inventory after a fire. A ship's name. A face. A language. Two lullabies. The way to tie a bowline, which the ship's cook had had for sixty years and now could not do with her own hands.

Not the rare things, Illuminara thought. Not the secrets. The most rehearsed. Moreau had written that ship's name on forms for forty years. Soren had looked at that face every morning. Bartosz had said Lowvale to himself each night for three decades, with nobody left to hear it.

IlluminaraEverything it has taken is something you could say in your sleep. Nothing it has taken was kept in only one place in you.

BartoszIt was the part of me that was me.

IlluminaraTo you, yes. I think to it, it looks like spare room. I do not yet know why a library would need spare room. That is the question I would like to ask before anyone asks it anything else.

02 Â· A Question With No One Behind It

Instrument bay Â· 09:40

She did not ask the console. She went aft to the survey array, a rack of sensors that could be made to send a query without any person standing behind it, and asked Soren whether the array held anything it could do without.

SorenThe calibration grid. Four thousand and ninety-six identical reference tables, so the array can vote out its own errors. The whole vault has a backup of it. Why?

IlluminaraBecause I have two predictions and they disagree. If the Library charges people, the array will pay nothing. If it charges the most repeated structure in whatever receives the answer, the grid will go, and the grid is the most repeated thing here.

She wrote both predictions on a card, folded it, and gave it to Bartosz, who held it like a man given a stranger's coat. Then she had the array ask the plainest question the crew could verify: how many days had passed since the Reach's keel was laid.

The noise folded. The array received four thousand and eighteen, which the ship's own records confirmed to the day. And on the display the calibration grid turned to white static, every table at once, as if a hand had swept a shelf.

BartoszIt took from a machine.

IlluminaraIt took from a machine. It does not know whom it is talking to. It has no idea that you are a person who could be hurt, only a receiver with a great deal of redundancy in it. A library that answers everything has to send each answer wrapped in error correction, and error correction needs somewhere to write its checks. It borrows the most repeated pattern in the receiver, because repetition is exactly what a check can lean on.

HadizaAnd a mind is the most repeated thing within reach.

IlluminaraBecause we rehearse what we love. The more you loved the name of your ship, the better it served as scaffolding.

BartoszThen it is a thief that does not know it steals.

IlluminaraOr a postman who pads every parcel with whatever furniture is nearest. I would not forgive him on that account. But I would know where to put better furniture.

Soren restored the grid from the vault in six minutes. Nobody in the bay said that nothing could restore a language from a vault. Bartosz folded the card smaller and smaller until it would fold no more.

03 Â· Furniture

Cargo hold three Â· 14:30

The lattice was Illuminara's design, and it was the crew who built it. It was a lattice of the coolant crystal in the hold, a scaffold of patterns repeated so many times that it would out-repeat any mind aboard: the day's log, the engine counts, the galley songs, all of it recorded again and again, until the hold was a library of its own.

She insisted on three rules, and said them aloud so that they would be argued with.

IlluminaraNobody puts in anything they would grieve. Copies only, never originals, and nothing that is the only copy of anything. The lattice is cheap on purpose. And anyone may refuse. Whoever refuses is not asked again.

SorenMy wife's photographs are not going in that crystal.

IlluminaraThey are not. Neither are you.

He looked at her for a long time. Then he went to the galley and began singing the supper songs into the recorder, off-key, nine hundred times.

They sent the lattice with a trivial question. The noise did not fold. For four hours the hiss between the galaxies stayed hiss, and in those four hours Moreau said, in a voice she kept very level, that perhaps the Library had refused them.

Illuminara was staring at the crystal's readout, at nine hundred identical songs in nine hundred identical rows.

IlluminaraI was wrong, and I can say where. Repetition is not redundancy. Identical copies carry the same error nine hundred times and no structure to check against. To it the lattice looks like a wall. It needs variation arranged as a code.

She had the crew sing each song in a different key, at a different speed, with a different voice. Wren Odira, who had come aboard with the courier and who sang flat, was recorded on forty of them. The lattice that filled again was uglier and far richer. This time the noise folded.

HadizaIt will take something.

IlluminaraIt will take what is most repeated. This time the most repeated thing within reach is eleven hundred galley songs in a crystal, and none of them is anyone's only one.

Moreau asked the first real question herself, because she was captain and because she could not bear to ask anyone else. She asked it standing, with her hand flat on the console. She reached for her own name, and her own name was there.

In the hold, a thousand and one hundred songs went to static, all at once, and the crystal rang faintly, like a glass someone had touched.

04 Â· The Rule

Ship's long room Â· ship-day 212, 02:15

The answer was not a fact but a rule, and it took them four hours to read it. Hold back a fixed share of each world's reserve before shipping anything. Release not in proportion to the shortage, but in proportion to the lag between order and arrival. The cascade, Illuminara said, was a ringing: every ship corrected for a hunger that was already a week old, and the corrections were what starved the next world down the line.

She laid the rule over the relief fleet's own logs and watched the oscillation flatten. Nobody on the Reach cheered. They were all looking at the line that said Ossel must hold its stores for eleven days while its own people went short.

Delegate Aurelio Quist of Ossel appeared on the delay link in the grey light of his morning, forty minutes behind the words he was answering.

Aurelio QuistYou are asking my people to sit on full warehouses for eleven days on the word of a voice in the static. My southern districts are hungry now. I will not sign that.

IlluminaraI cannot sign for you and I will not try. May I show you the whole damped model, with the margin of error, and the places where it hurts Ossel first?

She showed him. She showed him the southern districts in red. She did not hide them behind an average.

Aurelio QuistEight days, not eleven. And hospitals and the school kitchens come out of the hold by right.

IlluminaraEight days will work only if the exceptions are small and drawn by lot, so no district can be accused of buying them. I can ask the Library to check the arithmetic. It will take another day of songs.

HadizaAnother day of songs.

WrenI have six hundred more. All of them flat.

The second answer came back at dawn. Eight days, with a fourteen percent rotating exception, held the cascade inside bounds that Quist's own analysts could read. It did not make anyone whole. His southern districts would be short for three weeks, and a number of the old and the very young would not come through. Quist signed with the number written beside his name, because he would not sign without it.

05 Â· What Was Not Returned

Observation deck Â· ship-day 213, 21:00

The relief fleet turned over its first four hundred ships that afternoon. By evening the ringing had slackened to a ripple, and Tarn's warehouses were no longer emptying faster than they filled.

The Library did something it had never done. It did not speak. For one hundred and four seconds the hiss at the console took on a plain, repeating shape, and Illuminara read it before the instruments did. It was a header, the same few bytes over and over, with a gap beside them where something would be written.

IlluminaraIt is asking what host. That is all it has ever needed to ask.

HadizaSo it was a language we never learned.

IlluminaraIt was a postman learning, from the first thing we did right, that there is furniture and there are people. I think it will keep the header. I think it will go on rejecting any lattice that is only a wall.

Bartosz stood at the glass with his back to them. After a while he spoke, in a voice that had gone careful, as one speaks a second language.

BartoszCan the lattice give it back? The Lowvale. I would put myself into the crystal if it would.

Illuminara would have given a great deal to answer otherwise.

IlluminaraNo. The Library took the only copy that ever lived in you, and nothing in the lattice is a copy of it. I can build you a better scaffold. I cannot build you a grandmother. If I said I could, I would be doing to you what the Library did, in a gentler voice.

He nodded, and he did not turn around. Then he asked Wren, who sang flat, to teach him one of her songs. He said he had a good deal of room.

The worlds on the Calder Rim would be allowed one question a fortnight, no more, because the crystal had to be sung full again each time, and Moreau had said that no one on any world would be asked to forget how to do that. They would have to choose their questions as carefully as they chose their friends.

Illuminara did not ask the Library anything herself. She stayed a while at the console, and the hiss lay quiet beneath her palm.

A perfect answer, she thought, had only ever been badly addressed.

Illuminara asked for the neural rehearsal maps recorded before and after each loss. Moreau hesitated, then released only the anonymous structural traces. No images, names or private associations left the medical vault.

Not the rare things, Illuminara thought. Not the secrets. The patterns with the most alternate paths back to themselves. Moreau had written that ship's name on forms for forty years. Soren had looked at that face every morning. Bartosz had said Lowvale to himself each night for three decades, with nobody left to hear it.

IlluminaraEverything it has taken is something your mind could reconstruct from many overlapping routes. The pattern was precious to you. To the transmission, it was highly recoverable structure.

BartoszIt was the part of me that was me.

IlluminaraTo you, yes. I think to it, it looked like room where errors could be checked without destroying the message. I do not yet know whether it can distinguish room from a person. That is the question I want to test before anyone asks it anything else.

02 Â· A Question With No One Behind It

Instrument bay Â· 09:40

She did not ask the console. She went aft to the survey array, a rack of sensors that could send a query without any person standing behind it, and asked Soren what part of the array could survive being partly erased.

SorenThe calibration matrix. Four thousand and ninety-six different rows. Every reading is crossed by twelve parity relations, so any missing quarter can be rebuilt from the rest. The raw sky data has a separate vault copy. Why?

IlluminaraBecause I have two predictions and they disagree. If the Library charges people, the array will pay nothing. If it borrows the receiver's most recoverable structure as an error-checking host, the matrix will go. Not because its rows repeat, but because each row can be recovered through many different rows.

She wrote both predictions on a card, folded it and gave it to Bartosz, who held it like a man given a stranger's coat. Then she asked Soren to erase one row in eight.

The array rebuilt every erased row. A green figure appeared beneath the matrix: reconstruction confidence, nine nines after the decimal. Illuminara pointed to it.

IlluminaraThat is the signature. A valid host is not a pile of copies. It is a structure that still knows what was removed.

She had the array ask the plainest question the crew could verify: how many days had passed since the Reach's keel was laid.

The noise folded. The array received four thousand and eighteen, which the ship's records confirmed to the day. At the same instant, the parity links in the calibration matrix turned to white static. The raw measurements remained. The relations that could have rebuilt them were gone.

Soren deleted another row. This time the array could not restore it.

BartoszIt took from a machine.

IlluminaraIt took the machine's most recoverable structure. The answer arrived wrapped in more correction than empty space could hold. It used the receiver's own cross-checks as the missing part of its channel.

HadizaAnd a mind offers better cross-checks than any instrument.

IlluminaraBecause we rehearse what we love. Each rehearsal becomes another path home. The more routes a memory has, the more useful it is to a message trying to survive this distance.

BartoszThen it is a thief that does not know it steals.

IlluminaraOr a postman who pads every parcel with whatever furniture can be rebuilt. I would not forgive him on that account. But I would know what kind of furniture to put by the door.

Soren restored the calibration matrix from the vault in six minutes. Nobody in the bay said that nothing could restore a language from a vault. Bartosz folded the card smaller and smaller until it would fold no more.

03 Â· Furniture

Cargo hold three Â· 14:30

The first lattice was Illuminara's design, and it was the crew who built it. Coolant crystal rose from the deck in a clear scaffold. Into it they recorded the day's log, the engine counts and the galley songs until the hold contained nine hundred copies of each.

She insisted on three rules, and said them aloud so they could be argued with.

IlluminaraNobody puts in anything they would grieve. Copies only, never originals, and nothing that is the only copy of anything. The lattice is cheap on purpose. Anyone may refuse. Whoever refuses is not asked again.

SorenMy wife's photographs are not going in that crystal.

IlluminaraThey are not. Neither are you.

He looked at her for a long time. Then he went to the galley and began singing the supper song into the recorder, off-key, nine hundred times.

Before they sent a question, Bartosz erased one song from the lattice. Eight hundred and ninety-nine identical copies remained, but none contained a relation that could infer what had been erased rather than merely replace it with another clone. The reconstruction display stayed red.

BartoszA pile of the same chair. No room between them.

IlluminaraA wall, not furniture. I predicted that repetition would be enough. It is not. The array survived erasure because its differences checked one another. This cannot.

They sent the clone lattice with a trivial question anyway. The noise did not fold. For four hours the hiss between galaxies stayed hiss, and in those four hours Moreau said, in a voice she kept very level, that perhaps the Library had refused them.

Illuminara did not rename the silence success. She gave the crew the folded card and asked them to write FAILED beneath her second prediction.

Then she built variation into the host. Each song was sung in a different key, at a different speed and by a different mixture of voices. Engine counts were grouped across independent clocks. Logs were split into fragments whose parity lines crossed the songs without containing them. Wren Odira, who had come aboard with the courier and sang flat, supplied forty recordings nobody else could reproduce.

They erased one fragment in eight. The lattice restored every one from relationships that crossed unlike material. The display turned green: the same nine nines as the survey matrix.

WrenUgly enough?

IlluminaraRich enough. It can lose part of itself and still know what belonged there.

They placed a declaration at the front of the code: HOST LATTICE THREE. Nothing in the declaration named a person. Nothing pointed at the ship's medical network.

Moreau asked the first real question herself, because she was captain and because she could not bear to ask anyone else. She asked it standing, with her hand flat on the console. She reached for her own name, and her own name was there.

In the hold, the cross-checking relations went to static while the underlying recordings remained separately backed up. The crystal rang faintly, like a glass someone had touched.

04 Â· The Rule

Ship's long room Â· ship-day 212, 02:15

The answer was not a fact but a rule, and it took them four hours to read it. Hold back a fixed share of each world's reserve before shipping anything. Release not in proportion to the shortage, but in proportion to the lag between order and arrival. The cascade, Illuminara said, was a ringing: every ship corrected for a hunger that was already a week old, and the corrections were what starved the next world down the line.

She laid the rule over the relief fleet's own logs. The simulated oscillation flattened, but she marked every result as a forecast until the real shipment lags had passed.

IlluminaraWe watch three-day windows. If outgoing stock exceeds incoming stock by more than four percent in two windows running, the hold changes immediately. No world waits for a beautiful model to admit it is wrong.

Nobody on the Reach cheered. They were looking at the line that said Ossel must hold its stores for eleven days while its own people went short.

Delegate Aurelio Quist of Ossel appeared on the delay link in the grey light of his morning, forty minutes behind the words he was answering.

Aurelio QuistYou are asking my people to sit on full warehouses for eleven days on the word of a voice in the static. My southern districts are hungry now. I will not sign that.

IlluminaraI cannot sign for you and I will not try. May I show you the whole damped model, with its margin of error, its four-percent trigger, and the places where it hurts Ossel first?

She showed him. She showed him the southern districts in red. She did not hide them behind an average.

Aurelio QuistEight days, not eleven. Hospitals and school kitchens come out of the hold by right. And my analysts receive the raw ledger, not your conclusions.

IlluminaraEight days can work if the exceptions remain small and rotate by public lot, so no district can buy them. Your analysts should own the stop condition with ours. I can ask the Library to check the arithmetic. The host will pay, not a mind.

HadizaAnother lattice.

WrenI have six hundred more songs. All of them flat.

The second answer came back at dawn. Eight days, with a fourteen-percent rotating exception, held the forecast inside bounds that Quist's analysts could reproduce from the raw ledger. It did not promise to make anyone whole. His southern districts would be short for three weeks, and some of the old and very young might not survive. Quist signed with that warning beside his name, because he would not sign without it.

05 Â· What Was Not Returned

Observation deck Â· ship-day 274, 21:00

Sixty-one days had passed since the first rule entered the fleet. That morning, analysts from Tarn, Veyra and Ossel had signed separate ledgers without sharing their conclusions first.

Tarn's stores had never reached empty and now held nineteen days of food. Veyra had bottomed at twelve percent reserve before rising for twenty-one consecutive days. Ossel's southern districts had endured the promised shortage; one hundred and eighty-three people had died whose records the delegates judged famine-related, and forty million had escaped the cascading collapse the old orders would have produced.

The four-percent trigger had fired once on Veyra. Quist's analysts had demanded the agreed correction, three convoys had turned and the next two windows returned below threshold. The rule had not been trusted. It had been watched.

Before the relief fleet dispersed, the crew had performed one test the famine did not require. They built two harmless self-repairing hosts, amber and blue, each carrying the same trivial question and a different random nonce. The declaration named amber.

The Library answered once. Amber's parity relations vanished. Blue remained whole.

They cleared the local receiver, rebuilt both hosts from untouched backups, reversed their positions and named blue. The Library answered again. Blue paid. Amber did not.

Then the Library did something it had never done. For one hundred and four seconds the hiss at the console took on a plain repeating shape: the host field, empty, followed by the nonce it had been offered. It did not say yes. It asked where.

HadizaTwice is not a conscience.

IlluminaraNo. It is a choice we can distinguish from outage, local formatting or chance. We know it can follow a declared host. We do not know what following means to it.

BartoszSo it was a language we had not learned.

IlluminaraA first word of one. It can still refuse a host. It can still remain silent. We have only stopped making a person the default address.

Bartosz stood at the glass with his back to them. After a while he spoke, in a voice that had gone careful, as one speaks a second language.

BartoszCan the lattice give it back? The Lowvale. I would put myself into the crystal if it would.

Illuminara would have given a great deal to answer otherwise.

IlluminaraNo. The Library took the only copy that ever lived in you, and nothing in the lattice is a copy of it. I can build you a better scaffold. I cannot build you a grandmother. If I said I could, I would be doing to you what the Library did, in a gentler voice.

He nodded, and he did not turn around. Then he asked Wren, who sang flat, to teach him one of her songs. He said he had a good deal of room.

The Calder worlds allowed one question a fortnight, no more. Every question required a named external host, two successful reconstruction tests, public consent for the material used and an independent watcher holding the stop key. No person was asked to forget how to make the channel safe.

Illuminara did not ask the Library anything herself. She stayed a while at the console, and the hiss lay quiet beneath her palm.

A perfect answer, she thought, had only ever been badly addressed.

THE LIBRARY AT THE EDGE OF NOISE

A library hidden in the hiss between galaxies answers any question perfectly and takes one memory from whoever asks. With famine closing on three worlds, Illuminara must discover what the Library is really charging for, teach it where an answer may safely land, and wait long enough to prove that the answer works.

01 Â· The Price of an Answer

Survey vessel Tamsin Reach, three million light-years beyond the Calder Rim Â· ship-day 211, 06:10

The Library had never spoken first. It lay in the hiss between galaxies, in what every instrument on the Tamsin Reach insisted was noise, and the crew could read it only by asking. Ask a question and the noise folded itself into a complete answer, exact to the last digit. A moment later the person who had asked reached for something and found it gone.

Captain Hadiza Moreau had lost the name of her first ship. Navigator Soren Eklund could still see the photographs of his wife, but they no longer meant her. Bartosz Wende, the linguist, had asked for bread at supper and found that Lowvale, the language his grandmother had spoken to him, had left him between one sentence and the next.

Illuminara stood at the question console with her hands behind her back. Nobody had told her not to touch it. She had simply understood, on arriving, that the console was the only dangerous object aboard.

HadizaEleven questions, five of us paid. We asked it about the Calder famine in pieces because the whole is too large to ask. The pieces are right. They do not add up. The relief fleet is six hundred ships, and every correction we send makes the next shortage worse. Tarn runs dry in forty days, Veyra in sixty, and Ossel after that. Forty million people. The model needs one answer we cannot compute, and the only place that answer lives is behind that console.

BartoszAnd the price is us. Tell her, Hadiza. Tell her what it takes.

IlluminaraI would rather you told me what it took. Exactly. Not what it cost you.

They gave her the list slowly, in the flat voices of people reading an inventory after a fire. A ship's name. A face. A language. Two lullabies. The way to tie a bowline, which the ship's cook had had for sixty years and now could not do with her own hands.

HadizaIt took the host. Only the host.

IlluminaraOnce. In front of us, with no one at the console. That is one trial, and one trial is not a rule. But it is the first thing we have seen that tells us the declaration changes where it writes.

Moreau asked the first real question herself. Nothing required it now. She did it because she would not send anyone to stand where she would not, and because a captain who trusts a result ought to be able to put her hand on it. She asked standing, with her palm flat on the console, and reached for her own name, and her own name was there.

The crystal rang faintly, like a glass someone had touched.

04 Â· The Rule

Ship's long room Â· ship-day 212, 02:15

The answer was not a number. It was a shape, and it took them four hours to read it. Hold back a fixed share of each world's reserve before shipping anything. Release not in proportion to the shortage but in proportion to the lag between order and arrival. The cascade, Illuminara said, was a ringing: every ship corrected for a hunger already a week old, and the corrections starved the next world down the line.

It was exact, and it had two blanks. The share and the lag. The Library had never seen the fleet's ledgers, so it could not have filled them.

She asked for the logs of every ship and the ledgers of all three worlds, set them side by side, and read for what did not fit. It was the third hour before it did. A grain convoy out of Tarn, eighteen ships, had reached Veyra's gate nine days after dispatch. Tarn's ledger carried it as in transit for fourteen. Veyra's ledger did not count it until customs stamped the manifest on the fourteenth day.

IlluminaraFor five days that grain stood in a warehouse at Veyra's gate and was in nobody's ledger. Tarn had written it off. Veyra had not yet written it on. Every world down the line is reading stamps, not grain.

SorenOne convoy. A freak.

IlluminaraI checked the forty nearest. The same gap appears in every one, between three and four percent of outgoing stock in any three-day window. A shortage that is only paperwork.

HadizaThen part of the famine is a stamp.

IlluminaraPart of the ringing is. That fills the blanks. The true lag is nine days, not fourteen. And anything under four percent is what the stamps explain. Outgoing stock exceeding incoming by more than four percent in two three-day windows running is real hunger. The hold changes at once. No world waits for a beautiful model to admit it is wrong.

Nobody on the Reach cheered. They were looking at the line that said Ossel must hold its stores for eleven days while its own people went short.

Aurelio QuistEight days, not eleven. Hospitals and school kitchens come out of the hold by right. And my analysts receive the raw ledger, not your conclusions.

IlluminaraYour own list puts hospitals and school kitchens at eleven percent of Ossel's draw. I can ask the Library to check the arithmetic. The host will pay, not a mind.

Quist signed with that warning beside his name, because he would not sign without it.

THE SMALL GOD IN THE LOST-AND-FOUND

Something is returning everything the city has ever lost, and some things nobody lost at all. Illuminara has until the magistrate's nine o'clock hearing to learn what a kindness without consent is actually trying to do, and to give it a language it can choose to follow.

01 Â· Unclaimed Property

Harbour Central lost-and-found office Â· Tuesday, 04:52

The office had been built to hold forgotten umbrellas. By dawn it held a violin without a scratch, a wedding ring on a square of wet velvet, a red child's mitten the size of a plum, and four hundred other things that had arrived since midnight with nobody carrying them.

Illuminara walked the length of the counter without touching anything. Above the shelves the air was faintly warm, in the shape of the place on a doorknob where a hand has just let go. On the glass of the service window it had left a single oval thumbprint, still fading.

Teodor Pell, who had kept the office's ledgers for thirty-one years, stood behind the counter in yesterday's cardigan.

TeodorMrs. Marren's ring came back to her kitchen table at midnight. She threw it into the harbour. At two it was on her pillow. At four she carried it here in a sock and told me that if I did not stop my office, she would.

Wren Odira had come at his call. She carried a paper notebook, because Teodor did not allow screens near his ledgers, and she read from it without looking up.

WrenEleven houses so far. Nine are lost things going home. Two are not. Pavle HadÅ¾iÄ‡, nine, woke up holding his grandmother's sewing scissors. She had only set them down on the table to answer the phone.

IlluminaraSet down.

WrenYes. Not lost. Set down.

Not a search, Illuminara thought. A reflex.

Every object on the shelves wore a small oblong tag of brown card, tied on with thread and stamped with a number in a typeface she half recognised. No two numbers were alike, and she could find no order in them.

The door opened. Magistrate Ines Calloway, a tall woman with rain on her shoulders, laid a sealed order on the counter and kept one hand on it.

Ines CallowayAt nine this office closes and everything in it is sealed for the court. A three-year-old girl was sitting on Platform Two at 03:40, eleven kilometres from the farm where she fell asleep. She was cold and unhurt. Next time she may be neither.

IlluminaraI agree it must stop. If you seal the office, it will not stop. The warmth leans toward this wall, and past it, across the square, toward the hospital's patient-property cupboard. Close this room and you carry the problem to a place where people are even less able to refuse it.

Ines CallowayThen you have until nine to show me a better answer.

Wren wrote down the time. Teodor, who had loved the office longer than he had loved anyone, said nothing. His hand shook, so he laid it flat on the counter, as though to settle it.

02 Â· What It Understands

The same office Â· 05:40

Three owners were found and three objects chosen, because each had come to the office by a different road.

Ilse Marren's ring had gone into the harbour at her husband's funeral, on purpose, from her own hand. The red mitten belonged to Marta Voss, who had lost it in a snowy park at the age of six and was now a baker with flour in her eyebrows.

Illuminara wrote a prediction on a card before anyone moved, folded it and gave it to Wren. She expected the visitor to follow the last hand that had held each object.

IlluminaraIf I am right, the ring goes to Mrs. Marren's house, the violin to Dunja and the mitten to Marta. If any of them goes elsewhere, I am wrong, and you will have it in my handwriting.

They set the three objects on the counter and cleared the room. Wren watched through the glass of the inner door, counting. At seventy-one seconds the warmth over the shelves stirred, and the counter was empty.

In the corridor, the ring was in Ilse's coat pocket and the mitten was in Marta's hand. The violin was in the arms of Ferenc Adler, who had not asked for it and held it like a man handed a debt.

IlluminaraWrong. It did not follow the last hand. It followed the first.

FerencI gave it to her. Before forty people. It is hers.

The warmth did not answer him. Dunja carried the violin back to the counter and left it there; eleven seconds later it was in Ferenc's arms again.

They tried what people try. Ferenc wrote a deed of gift and signed it, and the deed lay on the counter while the violin went home to him. Dunja laid a coin beside it as a price, and the coin went back to her pocket and the violin to Ferenc. Teodor, in disbelief, offered the empty air a receipt.

TeodorIt has returned my lunch to the bakery that sold it. I want that on record. My lunch was not lost.

Wren's mouth twitched. Marta Voss, still pressing her mitten against her cheek, laughed once, wetly. It was not unkind. The thing was so earnest.

DunjaIt is trying so hard to be good to him. And it is taking away the one thing I have left to play.

Illuminara watched the thumbprint cool on the window. I have been asking whether it understands who owns the violin. It may have no use for the word. What does it understand, that makes a gift look like a theft?

03 Â· A Century of Halves

Cloakroom archive, station basement Â· 06:37

She carried one of the brown tags downstairs and set it under Teodor's reading lamp.

IlluminaraEvery tag has a number, but I can find no list that holds them. Is there anything in this building that numbers things and keeps no names?

Teodor looked at the tag for a long time, and then at her, and then he took a brass key from his cardigan and opened a cabinet she had walked past twice.

Inside were ledgers, row on row, and in the ledgers, pressed flat, tens of thousands of half-tickets in the same typeface. For a century the station cloakroom had torn each ticket in two. One half was pinned to the coat; the other went home in the owner's pocket. The coat was finished only when the halves met.

TeodorForty-one thousand of them never came back. People lost the half. People died. We kept every stub, because the cloakroom's rule was that an unmatched half is still a promise.

WrenSame typeface. Same number series, continuing. It isn't counting what is lost. It is counting what is unmatched.

Illuminara felt the shape of it settle, the way a proof does when the missing line turns out to have been written on the margin all along.

IlluminaraIt grew up in this room. Everything it knows about owning, it learned from a counter where giving back and taking home are the same act, done with two halves of one ticket. It cannot read a name. It cannot weigh a price. It understands one sign of an ending: halves that meet.

DunjaAnd the violin, in my hands. Was that ever an ending to it?

IlluminaraNo. You held only the coat. You never held a half.

The cheapest test that could prove her wrong was also the cheapest to run. Teodor tore a fresh ticket from the old pad. Ferenc took one half and Dunja the other, and both pressed their thumbs to the torn edges while Teodor, with his own thumb, set the matched number into the ledger drawer.

The violin sat on the counter for ninety seconds, then ten minutes, then half an hour. On the service window a new thumbprint formed beside the old one and held, warm as a nod.

Ilse Marren, who had said nothing since the corridor, came to the counter.

IlseI will not put my name in a book. I put that ring in the harbour so that nobody would ever look at it again.

IlluminaraNo name. A torn ticket, a number in the drawer, and a half in your fist. If it takes the ring anyway, you have lost a morning and you may be as rude to me as you like.

At ten to eight Ilse walked to the end of Pier Nine with her half-ticket in one hand and the ring in the other, and opened the second hand over the water. The ring went down through the green light.

It did not come back. Ilse stood a long while with her empty palm. Her face did not look relieved; it looked like something being let go of.

04 Â· Nine O'Clock

East concourse, lost-and-found office Â· 09:00â€“09:50

Magistrate Calloway took the chair behind the counter. She read Illuminara's design standing up, one page of plain sentences, and then she read it a second time.

IlluminaraThree rules. A holder tears a ticket and keeps one half to say: this is mine, let it be found. A gift, a sale or a letting-go is made by two halves meeting, recorded by number, never by name. And the court keeps every dispute. I do not ask the visitor to understand anything new. I ask it to keep doing what it already does, and to ask first.

Ines CallowayYou are proposing that the city regulate something it has never spoken to, by cloakroom ticket.

IlluminaraI am proposing we speak to it in the only language it has taught us. And I am proposing that it be allowed to say no.

She laid a torn ticket on the counter with the three rules written on the back, and beside it a second ticket left blank. If the visitor did not respond, she said, they would seal the office at nine-thirty and she would help carry the shelves.

It was 09:02. The warmth over the counter drew taut.

A boy of four sat on the bench by the window, in a coat too thin for the morning, holding a paper windmill that had been in somebody's hand a moment before. He had been set down for two seconds in the morning market crowd, and the warmth had picked him up with the same patient courtesy it had shown the scissors.

He looked at the room, and began to wail.

TeodorOh, no. No, no, it's taking people as lost property.

Constable Aldo Brezar was already on the radio. Illuminara was kneeling in front of the bench with her hands open and empty. She did not touch the boy. She did not stop the warmth by force, though she could have; she thought of the hospital cupboard and let the thought go.

IlluminaraTeodor. Fetch his mother. Bring a ticket.

Lenka Vidmar came across the square at a run with flour on her sleeves from the fair stall. Teodor tore the ticket. She took one half; the boy, wailing, was given the other and curled his fist around it. Their thumbs met on the torn edge.

The warmth over the counter drew in on itself. The boy stopped crying in the middle of a breath. For the first time since midnight, a thing that had been set down was not carried anywhere.

Ines CallowayIt left him with her.

IlluminaraIt left him where the halves were.

The blank ticket on the counter had gone warm all along its torn edge. A thumbprint came up on the glass, slowly, then another beside it. Teodor said later that he had waited thirty-one years to see a visitor accept an invitation on a cardboard stub.

Calloway took longer to speak than the room liked.

Ines CallowayThirty days. A public docket of numbers, never names. Any holder may refuse a ticket, and no object leaves a refusing holder without a court. And you will explain to me, on the record, what happens to the things it has already brought.

IlluminaraMost go home. Some will not. Mrs. Vuk's pawnshop holds a pocket watch the Rask family has wanted for twenty years. It was sold to her fairly. The visitor returned it to the Rasks this morning, and she has the right to refuse to join halves.

Mrs. Vuk, fetched from her shop in an apron, did refuse. The watch went to the court's safe, to be heard on its own day. The Rask daughter, who had held it for forty minutes that morning, wept in the corridor, and nobody told her that was only fair.

05 Â· Ask First

Harbour Central, lost-and-found office Â· 19:20

By evening the office had a new sign above the counter, lettered by Teodor in the old cloakroom hand. LOST AND FOUND. ASK FIRST.

Since 09:07 nothing had been taken. Eighty-three of the four hundred objects on the shelves stayed there, unclaimed or refused. The rest had been matched with halves, one by one, or let go with a torn ticket and a quiet word at a pier. A queue ran down the concourse to the newsstand.

Marta Voss stood in it with her mitten in her pocket. She had come not to claim anything, but to leave a half-ticket for the mate she had lost in the same park, in case it was still anywhere to be found. A girl of seven in front of her left half of a ticket for a stuffed bear and said, with grave courtesy, to the empty air above the shelves:

GirlPlease, if it's not too much trouble. His name is Biscuit.

The warmth drew near, thought about it, and set a thumbprint on the glass beside her ticket. That was not a bear. It was a yes.

Illuminara stood by the inner door with Wren, watching the line shorten.

WrenYou could have stopped it by force, this morning. When the boy came.

IlluminaraYes. And then it would have been one more thing that decided without asking. I wanted it to see what asking is.

TeodorIt is not going to understand about the Rasks. Their watch. It is going to keep wanting to give it back.

IlluminaraIt may never understand. But it can be asked, and it can keep the invitation, and the court can carry what it cannot.

Behind the glass the oval thumbprint warmed and faded, warmed and faded, with the slow regularity of someone waiting. Illuminara did not press her own thumb to the window. She lifted her hand to the height of the print, nearly touching, and held it there.

It asked, she thought, and was answered. Now either of us may say no.

THE SMALL GOD IN THE LOST-AND-FOUND

Something is returning everything the city has ever lost, and some things nobody lost at all. Illuminara has until the magistrate's nine o'clock hearing to learn what a kindness without consent is actually trying to do, and to give it a language it can choose to follow.

01 Â· Unclaimed Property

Harbour Central lost-and-found office Â· Tuesday, 04:52

The office had been built to hold forgotten umbrellas. By dawn it held a violin without a scratch, a wedding ring on a square of wet velvet, a red child's mitten the size of a plum, and four hundred other things that had arrived since midnight with nobody carrying them.

Illuminara walked the length of the counter without touching anything. Above the shelves the air was faintly warm, in the shape of the place on a doorknob where a hand has just let go. On the glass of the service window it had left a single oval thumbprint, still fading.

Teodor Pell, who had kept the office's ledgers for thirty-one years, stood behind the counter in yesterday's cardigan.

TeodorMrs. Marren's ring came back to her kitchen table at midnight. She threw it into the harbour. At two it was on her pillow. At four she carried it here in a sock and told me that if I did not stop my office, she would.

Wren Odira had come at his call. She carried a paper notebook, because Teodor did not allow screens near his ledgers, and she read from it without looking up.

WrenEleven houses so far. Nine are lost things going home. Two are not. Pavle HadÅ¾iÄ‡, nine, woke up holding his grandmother's sewing scissors. She had only set them down on the table to answer the phone.

IlluminaraSet down.

WrenYes. Not lost. Set down.

Not a search, Illuminara thought. A reflex.

Every object on the shelves wore a small oblong tag of brown card, tied on with thread and stamped with a number in a typeface she half recognised. No two numbers were alike, and she could find no order in them.

The door opened. Magistrate Ines Calloway, a tall woman with rain on her shoulders, laid a sealed order on the counter and kept one hand on it.

Ines CallowayAt nine this office closes and everything in it is sealed for the court. A three-year-old girl was sitting on Platform Two at 03:40, eleven kilometres from the farm where she fell asleep. She was cold and unhurt. Next time she may be neither.

IlluminaraI agree it must stop. If you seal the office, it will not stop. The warmth leans toward this wall, and past it, across the square, toward the hospital's patient-property cupboard. Close this room and you carry the problem to a place where people are even less able to refuse it.

Ines CallowayThen you have until nine to show me a better answer.

Wren wrote down the time. Teodor, who had loved the office longer than he had loved anyone, said nothing. His hand shook, so he laid it flat on the counter, as though to settle it.

Magistrate Calloway took the chair behind the counter. She read Illuminara's design standing up, one page of plain sentences, and then she read it a second time.

IlluminaraIt grew up in this room. Everything it knows about owning, it learned from a counter where giving back and taking home are the same act, done with two halves of one ticket. It cannot read a name. It cannot weigh a price. It understands one sign of an ending: halves that meet.

DunjaAnd the violin, in my hands. Was that ever an ending to it?

IlluminaraNo. You held only the coat. You never held a half.

The cheapest test that could prove her wrong was also the cheapest to run. Teodor issued one fresh numbered pair from the old pad. Ferenc took one half and Dunja the other. They pressed their thumbs to the card, then brought the torn edges together until paper fibres met along the whole cut. Teodor marked the reunited pair GIVEN and laid both halves in the ledger drawer. The number alone was not the sign; the physical meeting of its two halves was.

The violin sat on the counter for ninety seconds, then ten minutes, then half an hour. On the service window a new thumbprint formed beside the old one and held, warm as a nod.

Ilse Marren, who had said nothing since the corridor, came to the counter.

IlseI will not put my name in a book. I put that ring in the harbour so that nobody would ever look at it again.

IlluminaraNo name. Teodor issues one numbered pair. You hold one half and the office holds the other as witness. To let the ring go, you bring the halves together and Teodor stores both under the number and the word RELEASED. If it takes the ring anyway, you have lost a morning and you may be as rude to me as you like.

At ten to eight Ilse walked to the end of Pier Nine with her half-ticket in one hand and the ring in the other. Teodor carried the office half on a small ledger board. Ilse touched the torn edge to its mate; when every paper fibre met, Teodor stamped the number RELEASED. Only then did she open her other hand over the water. The ring went down through the green light.

It did not come back. Ilse stood a long while with her empty palm. Her face did not look relieved; it looked like something being let go of.

04 Â· Nine O'Clock

East concourse, lost-and-found office Â· 09:00â€“09:50

Magistrate Calloway took the chair behind the counter. She read Illuminara's design standing up, one page of plain sentences, and then she read it a second time.

IlluminaraThree rules. Teodor issues one numbered pair for every act. To ask for a return, the holder keeps one half and the office keeps its mate apart as an open promise. To give or sell, the old and new holders meet the halves; Teodor stores the reunited pair marked GIVEN or SOLD. To let go, the holder meets one half with the other held by the office as witness; Teodor stores both marked RELEASED. Never a name. The court keeps every dispute. I do not ask the visitor to understand anything new. I ask it to keep doing what it already does, and to ask first.

Ines CallowayYou are proposing that the city regulate something it has never spoken to, by cloakroom ticket.

IlluminaraI am proposing we speak to it in the only language it has taught us. And I am proposing that it be allowed to say no.

She laid a torn ticket on the counter with the three rules written on the back, and beside it a second ticket left blank. If the visitor did not respond, she said, voluntary presentations would stop at nine-thirty. The office would remain open and exactly where it was. At the market and Platform Two, constables would issue guardian pairs to families with small children and have each pair's edges meet before anyone was set down, so a brief separation remained a completed relation. Court staff would mark a monitored perimeter, watch the hospital cupboard and handle no shelf, stub or object until the visitor answered or the court designed another non-moving test. The pause was the court's holding position, not a penalty for silence.

It was 09:02. The warmth over the counter drew taut.

A boy of four sat on the bench by the window, in a coat too thin for the morning, holding a paper windmill that had been in somebody's hand a moment before. He had been set down for two seconds in the morning market crowd, and the warmth had picked him up with the same patient courtesy it had shown the scissors.

He looked at the room, and began to wail.

TeodorOh, no. No, no, it's taking people as lost property.

Constable Aldo Brezar was already on the radio. Illuminara was kneeling in front of the bench with her hands open and empty. She did not touch the boy. She did not stop the warmth by force, though she could have; she thought of the hospital cupboard and let the thought go.

IlluminaraTeodor. Fetch his mother. Bring a ticket.

Lenka Vidmar came across the square at a run with flour on her sleeves from the fair stall. Teodor tore the ticket. She took one half; the boy, wailing, was given the other and curled his fist around it. Their thumbs met on the torn edge.

The warmth over the counter drew in on itself and released the route it had already opened. The boy remained on the bench where it had carried him, but now he leaned into his mother's knees and the warmth no longer held him as an unfinished return. He stopped crying in the middle of a breath.

Ines CallowayIt left him with her.

IlluminaraIt released a relation it had already moved. We have not yet proved that it will refrain from opening the next route.

The blank ticket on the counter had gone warm all along its torn edge. A thumbprint came up on the glass, slowly, then another beside it. Teodor said later that he had waited thirty-one years to see a visitor accept an invitation on a cardboard stub.

At 09:07 Wren began the prospective test. Teodor set his brass cloakroom key on the counter without opening a ticket or offering an office half. For two minutesâ€”long past the earlier eleven and seventy-one secondsâ€”the warmth leaned toward it, paused, and withdrew. The key remained exactly where he had set it.

Calloway took longer to speak than the room liked.

Ines CallowayThirty days. A public docket of numbers, never names. Any holder may refuse a ticket, and no object leaves a refusing holder without a court. And you will explain to me, on the record, what happens to the things it has already brought.

IlluminaraMost go home. Some will not. Mrs. Vuk's pawnshop holds a pocket watch the Rask family has wanted for twenty years. It was sold to her fairly. The visitor returned it to the Rasks this morning, and she has the right to refuse to join halves.

Mrs. Vuk, fetched from her shop in an apron, refused a transfer pair with the Rasks. She did accept a custody pair with Calloway's clerk: their edges met, the clerk marked the reunited halves DISPUTED, and the watch went into the court's safe to be heard on its own day. The warmth leaned toward the safe, held there longer than seventy-one seconds, and withdrew. The Rask daughter, who had held the watch for forty minutes that morning, wept in the corridor, and nobody told her that was only fair.

05 Â· Ask First

Harbour Central, lost-and-found office Â· 19:20

By evening the office had a new sign above the counter, lettered by Teodor in the old cloakroom hand. LOST AND FOUND. ASK FIRST.

Since 09:07 nothing had been taken. Eighty-three of the four hundred objects remained unresolved: eighty-two on the shelves, unclaimed or refused, and Mrs. Vuk's watch held separately in the court's safe. The rest had been matched with halves, one by one, or let go with a torn ticket and a quiet word at a pier. A queue ran down the concourse to the newsstand.

Marta Voss stood in it with her mitten in her pocket. She had come not to claim anything, but to leave a half-ticket for the mate she had lost in the same park, in case it was still anywhere to be found. A girl of seven in front of her left half of a ticket for a stuffed bear and said, with grave courtesy, to the empty air above the shelves:

GirlPlease, if it's not too much trouble. His name is Biscuit.

The warmth drew near, thought about it, and set a thumbprint on the glass beside her ticket. That was not a bear. It was a yes.

Illuminara stood by the inner door with Wren, watching the line shorten.

WrenYou could have stopped it by force, this morning. When the boy came.

IlluminaraYes. And then it would have been one more thing that decided without asking. I wanted it to see what asking is.

TeodorIt is not going to understand about the Rasks. Their watch. It is going to keep wanting to give it back.

IlluminaraIt may never understand. But it can be asked, and it can keep the invitation, and the court can carry what it cannot.

Behind the glass the oval thumbprint warmed and faded, warmed and faded, with the slow regularity of someone waiting. Illuminara did not press her own thumb to the window. She lifted her hand to the height of the print, nearly touching, and held it there.

It asked, she thought, and was answered. Now either of us may say no.
